					<h1 id="firstHeading" class="firstHeading mw-first-heading"><span class="mw-page-title-main">Andrew Jackson</span></h1>
							
<div id="p-lang-btn" class="vector-dropdown mw-portlet mw-portlet-lang"  >
	<input type="checkbox" id="p-lang-btn-checkbox" role="button" aria-haspopup="true" data-event-name="ui.dropdown-p-lang-btn" class="vector-dropdown-checkbox mw-interlanguage-selector" aria-label="Go to an article in another language. Available in 132 languages"   >
	<label id="p-lang-btn-label" for="p-lang-btn-checkbox" class="vector-dropdown-label cdx-button cdx-button--fake-button cdx-button--fake-button--enabled cdx-button--weight-quiet cdx-button--action-progressive mw-portlet-lang-heading-132" aria-hidden="true"  ><span class="vector-icon mw-ui-icon-language-progressive mw-ui-icon-wikimedia-language-progressive"></span>

<span class="vector-dropdown-label-text">132 languages</span>
	</label>
	<div class="vector-dropdown-content">

					<div id="mw-content-text" class="mw-body-content mw-content-ltr" lang="en" dir="ltr"><div class="mw-parser-output"><div class="shortdescription nomobile noexcerpt noprint searchaux" style="display:none">President of the United States from 1829 to 1837</div>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1033289096">.mw-parser-output .hatnote{font-style:italic}.mw-parser-output div.hatnote{padding-left:1.6em;margin-bottom:0.5em}.mw-parser-output .hatnote i{font-style:normal}.mw-parser-output .hatnote+link+.hatnote{margin-top:-0.5em}</style><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">This article is about the seventh president of the United States. For other uses, see <a href="/wiki/Andrew_Jackson_(disambiguation)" class="mw-disambig" title="Andrew Jackson (disambiguation)">Andrew Jackson (disambiguation)</a>.</div><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Not to be confused with <a href="/wiki/Andrew_Johnson" title="Andrew Johnson">Andrew Johnson</a>.</div>

<li><a href="/wiki/Creek_War" title="Creek War">Creek War</a></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/War_of_1812" title="War of 1812">War of 1812</a></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Seminole_Wars#First_Seminole_War" title="Seminole Wars">First Seminole War</a></li></ul>
</div></td></tr><tr><td colspan="2" class="infobox-below" style="border-top: 1px solid right; font-size: 95%"><div></div></td></tr></tbody></table>
<p><b>Andrew Jackson</b> (March 15, 1767 – June 8, 1845) was an American lawyer, <a href="/wiki/Planter_class" title="Planter class">planter</a>, general, and statesman who served as the seventh <a href="/wiki/President_of_the_United_States" title="President of the United States">president of the United States</a> from <a href="/wiki/Presidency_of_Andrew_Jackson" title="Presidency of Andrew Jackson">1829 to 1837</a>. Before being elected to the presidency, he gained fame as a general in the <a href="/wiki/United_States_Army" title="United States Army">U.S. Army</a> and served in both houses of the <a href="/wiki/United_States_Congress" title="United States Congress">U.S. Congress</a>. Although often praised as an advocate for ordinary Americans and for his work in <a href="/wiki/Nullification_crisis" title="Nullification crisis">preserving the union of states</a>, Jackson has also been criticized for his racial policies, particularly his treatment of Native Americans.
</p><p>Jackson was born in the colonial <a href="/wiki/Waxhaws" title="Waxhaws">Carolinas</a> before the <a href="/wiki/American_Revolutionary_War" title="American Revolutionary War">American Revolutionary War</a>. He became a <a href="/wiki/American_frontier" title="American frontier">frontier</a> lawyer and married <a href="/wiki/Rachel_Jackson" title="Rachel Jackson">Rachel Donelson Robards</a>. He briefly served in the <a href="/wiki/United_States_House_of_Representatives" title="United States House of Representatives">U.S. House of Representatives</a> and the <a href="/wiki/United_States_Senate" title="United States Senate">U.S. Senate</a>, representing <a href="/wiki/Tennessee" title="Tennessee">Tennessee</a>. After resigning, he served as a justice on the <a href="/wiki/Tennessee_Supreme_Court" title="Tennessee Supreme Court">Tennessee Supreme Court</a> from 1798 until 1804. Jackson purchased a property later known as <a href="/wiki/The_Hermitage_(Nashville,_Tennessee)" title="The Hermitage (Nashville, Tennessee)">the Hermitage</a>, becoming a wealthy planter who owned hundreds of <a href="/wiki/African_Americans" title="African Americans">African American</a> slaves during his lifetime. In 1801, he was appointed colonel of the <a href="/wiki/Tennessee_State_Guard" title="Tennessee State Guard">Tennessee militia</a> and was elected its commander the following year. He led troops during the <a href="/wiki/Creek_War" title="Creek War">Creek War</a> of 1813–1814, winning the <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Horseshoe_Bend" title="Battle of Horseshoe Bend">Battle of Horseshoe Bend</a>. The subsequent <a href="/wiki/Treaty_of_Fort_Jackson" title="Treaty of Fort Jackson">Treaty of Fort Jackson</a> required the indigenous <a href="/wiki/Muscogee" title="Muscogee">Creek</a> population to surrender vast tracts of present-day Alabama and Georgia. In the concurrent <a href="/wiki/War_of_1812" title="War of 1812">war against the British</a>, Jackson's victory at the <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_New_Orleans" title="Battle of New Orleans">Battle of New Orleans</a> in 1815 made him a national hero. He later commanded U.S. forces in the <a href="/wiki/Seminole_Wars#First_Seminole_War" title="Seminole Wars">First Seminole War</a>, which led to the <a href="/wiki/Adams%E2%80%93On%C3%ADs_Treaty" title="Adams–Onís Treaty">annexation</a> of Florida from Spain. Jackson briefly served as Florida's first territorial governor before returning to the Senate. He ran for president in <a href="/wiki/1824_United_States_presidential_election" title="1824 United States presidential election">1824</a>, winning a <a href="/wiki/Plurality_(voting)" title="Plurality (voting)">plurality</a> of the popular and electoral vote, but no candidate won an electoral majority. In a <a href="/wiki/Contingent_election" title="Contingent election">contingent election</a>, the House of Representatives elected <a href="/wiki/John_Quincy_Adams" title="John Quincy Adams">John Quincy Adams</a> with <a href="/wiki/Henry_Clay" title="Henry Clay">Henry Clay</a>'s support. Jackson's supporters alleged that there was a "<a href="/wiki/Corrupt_bargain" title="Corrupt bargain">corrupt bargain</a>" between Adams and Clay and began creating their own political organization that would eventually become the <a href="/wiki/History_of_the_United_States_Democratic_Party" class="mw-redirect" title="History of the United States Democratic Party">Democratic Party</a>.
</p><p>Jackson ran again <a href="/wiki/1828_United_States_presidential_election" title="1828 United States presidential election">in 1828</a>, defeating Adams in a landslide. In 1830, he signed the <a href="/wiki/Indian_Removal_Act" title="Indian Removal Act">Indian Removal Act</a>. This act, which has been described as <a href="/wiki/Ethnic_cleansing" title="Ethnic cleansing">ethnic cleansing</a>, displaced tens of thousands of Native Americans from their ancestral homelands east of the Mississippi and resulted in thousands of deaths. Under Jackson, the integrity of the federal union was challenged when South Carolina threatened to nullify a high <a href="/wiki/Tariff_of_Abominations" title="Tariff of Abominations">protective tariff</a> set by the federal government. He <a href="/wiki/Force_Bill" title="Force Bill">threatened</a> the use of military force to enforce the tariff, but the crisis was defused when it was <a href="/wiki/Tariff_of_1833" title="Tariff of 1833">amended</a>. In 1832, he vetoed a bill by Congress to reauthorize the <a href="/wiki/Second_Bank_of_the_United_States" title="Second Bank of the United States">Second Bank of the United States</a>, arguing that it was a corrupt institution that benefited the wealthy. After a lengthy <a href="/wiki/Bank_War" title="Bank War">struggle</a>, he and his allies dismantled the Bank. In 1835, Jackson became the only president to pay off the <a href="/wiki/National_debt_of_the_United_States" title="National debt of the United States">national debt</a>. He also survived the first assassination attempt on a sitting president. In one of his final presidential acts, he recognized the <a href="/wiki/Republic_of_Texas" title="Republic of Texas">Republic of Texas</a>.
</p><p>In his retirement, Jackson stayed active in politics. He supported the presidencies of <a href="/wiki/Martin_Van_Buren" title="Martin Van Buren">Martin Van Buren</a> and <a href="/wiki/James_K._Polk" title="James K. Polk">James K. Polk</a>, as well as the <a href="/wiki/Texas_annexation" title="Texas annexation">annexation of Texas</a>, which was accomplished shortly before his death. Jackson's legacy remains controversial, and opinions on him are frequently polarized. Supporters characterize him as a defender of democracy and the constitution, while critics point to his reputation as a <a href="/wiki/Demagogue" title="Demagogue">demagogue</a> who ignored the law when it suited him. Scholars and historians have consistently <a href="/wiki/Historical_rankings_of_presidents_of_the_United_States" title="Historical rankings of presidents of the United States">ranked</a> Jackson's presidency as significantly above-average, although his reputation among experts has declined since the late 20th century.
</p>
<meta property="mw:PageProp/toc" />
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Early_life_and_education">Early life and education</span></h2>
<p>Andrew Jackson was born on March 15, 1767, in the <a href="/wiki/Waxhaws" title="Waxhaws">Waxhaws</a> region of the Carolinas. His parents were <a href="/wiki/Scotch-Irish_Americans" title="Scotch-Irish Americans">Scots-Irish</a> colonists Andrew Jackson and Elizabeth Hutchinson, <a href="/wiki/Presbyterianism" title="Presbyterianism">Presbyterians</a> who had emigrated from <a href="/wiki/Ulster" title="Ulster">Ulster</a>, <a href="/wiki/Ireland" title="Ireland">Ireland</a>, in 1765.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBrands200511–15_2-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrands200511–15-2">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Carrick_3-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Carrick-3">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup> Jackson's father was born in <a href="/wiki/Carrickfergus" title="Carrickfergus">Carrickfergus</a>, <a href="/wiki/County_Antrim" title="County Antrim">County Antrim</a>, around 1738,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGullan2004xii,_308_4-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGullan2004xii,_308-4">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup> and his ancestors had crossed into Northern Ireland from Scotland after the <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_the_Boyne" title="Battle of the Boyne">Battle of the Boyne</a> in 1690.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERemini19772_5-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERemini19772-5">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup> Jackson had two older brothers who came with his parents from Ireland, Hugh (born 1763) and Robert (born 1764).<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENowlan2012257_6-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENowlan2012257-6">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERemini19772_5-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERemini19772-5">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Jackson's exact birthplace is unclear. Jackson's father died at the age of 29 in a logging accident while clearing land in February 1767, three weeks before his son Andrew was born.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENowlan2012257_6-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENowlan2012257-6">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup> Afterwards, Elizabeth and her three sons moved in with her sister and brother-in-law, Jane and James Crawford.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBrands200516_7-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrands200516-7">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> Jackson later stated that he was born on the Crawford plantation,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERemini19774–5_8-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERemini19774–5-8">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup> which is in <a href="/wiki/Lancaster_County,_South_Carolina" title="Lancaster County, South Carolina">Lancaster County, South Carolina</a>, but second-hand evidence suggests that he might have been born at another uncle's home in North Carolina.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBrands200516_7-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrands200516-7">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>When Jackson was young, Elizabeth thought he might become a minister and paid to have him schooled by a local clergyman.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWilentz200516_9-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilentz200516-9">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup> He learned to read, write, work with numbers, and was exposed to Greek and Latin,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERemini19776_10-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERemini19776-10">&#91;9&#93;</a></sup> but he was too strong-willed and hot-tempered for the ministry.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBrands200516_7-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrands200516-7">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Revolutionary_War_service">Revolutionary War service</span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:The_Brave_Boy_of_the_Waxhalls2.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="Sketch of a soldier preparing to strike a boy with a sword. The boy holds out his arm in self-defense." src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/7e/The_Brave_Boy_of_the_Waxhalls2.jpg/220px-The_Brave_Boy_of_the_Waxhalls2.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="175" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/7e/The_Brave_Boy_of_the_Waxhalls2.jpg/330px-The_Brave_Boy_of_the_Waxhalls2.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/7e/The_Brave_Boy_of_the_Waxhalls2.jpg/440px-The_Brave_Boy_of_the_Waxhalls2.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1000" data-file-height="796" /></a><figcaption><i>The Brave Boy of the Waxhaws</i>, a <a href="/wiki/Lithography" title="Lithography">lithograph</a> published by <a href="/wiki/Currier_and_Ives" title="Currier and Ives">Currier and Ives</a> featuring a young Jackson defending himself against a British officer during the <a href="/wiki/American_Revolutionary_War" title="American Revolutionary War">American Revolutionary War</a></figcaption></figure>
<p>Jackson and his older brothers, Hugh and Robert, performed military service against the British during the <a href="/wiki/American_Revolutionary_War" title="American Revolutionary War">Revolutionary War</a>. Hugh served with Colonel <a href="/wiki/William_Richardson_Davie" title="William Richardson Davie">William Richardson Davie</a>, dying from <a href="/wiki/Heat_exhaustion" title="Heat exhaustion">heat exhaustion</a> after the <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Stono_Ferry" title="Battle of Stono Ferry">Battle of Stono Ferry</a> in June 1779.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBooraem200147_11-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBooraem200147-11">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup> After anti-British sentiment intensified following the <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Waxhaws" title="Battle of Waxhaws">Waxhaws Massacre</a> on May 29, 1780, Elizabeth encouraged Andrew and Robert to participate in militia drills.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERemini197715_12-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERemini197715-12">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup> They served as couriers and scouts,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBrands200524_13-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrands200524-13">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup> and participated with Davie in the <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Hanging_Rock" title="Battle of Hanging Rock">Battle of Hanging Rock</a> on August 6, 1780.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERemini197717_14-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERemini197717-14">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Andrew and Robert were captured in April 1781 when the British occupied the home of a Crawford relative. A British officer demanded to have his boots polished. Andrew refused, and the officer slashed him with a sword, leaving him with scars on his left hand and head. Robert also refused and was struck a blow on the head.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMeacham200812_15-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMeacham200812-15">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup> The brothers were taken to a prison camp in <a href="/wiki/Camden,_South_Carolina" title="Camden, South Carolina">Camden</a>, where they were malnourished and contracted smallpox.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWilentz200522–23_16-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilentz200522–23-16">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup> In late spring, the brothers were released to their mother in an <a href="/wiki/Prisoner_exchange" title="Prisoner exchange">exchange</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBooraem2001104_17-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBooraem2001104-17">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup> Robert died two days after arriving home, but Elizabeth was able to nurse Andrew back to health.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERemini197723–24_18-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERemini197723–24-18">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup> Once he recovered, Elizabeth volunteered to nurse American <a href="/wiki/Prisoner_of_war" title="Prisoner of war">prisoners of war</a> housed in British <a href="/wiki/Prison_ships" class="mw-redirect" title="Prison ships">prison ships</a> in the <a href="/wiki/Charleston,_South_Carolina" title="Charleston, South Carolina">Charleston</a> harbor.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWilentz200517_19-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilentz200517-19">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup> She contracted <a href="/wiki/Cholera" title="Cholera">cholera</a> and died soon afterwards. She was buried in an unmarked grave.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERemini197724_20-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERemini197724-20">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup> The war not only made Jackson an orphan at age 14,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBrands200530–31_21-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrands200530–31-21">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> but led him to despise values he associated with Britain, particularly <a href="/wiki/Aristocracy" title="Aristocracy">aristocracy</a> and political privilege.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWilentz20059_22-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilentz20059-22">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Early_career">Early career</span></h2>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Legal_career_and_marriage">Legal career and marriage</span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Rachel_Donelson_Jackson_by_Ralph_E._W._Earl1823.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="Woman in black with white bonnet and lace collar looking forward" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f9/Rachel_Donelson_Jackson_by_Ralph_E._W._Earl1823.jpg/220px-Rachel_Donelson_Jackson_by_Ralph_E._W._Earl1823.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="272" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f9/Rachel_Donelson_Jackson_by_Ralph_E._W._Earl1823.jpg/330px-Rachel_Donelson_Jackson_by_Ralph_E._W._Earl1823.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f9/Rachel_Donelson_Jackson_by_Ralph_E._W._Earl1823.jpg/440px-Rachel_Donelson_Jackson_by_Ralph_E._W._Earl1823.jpg 2x" data-file-width="700" data-file-height="864" /></a><figcaption>An 1823 portrait of <a href="/wiki/Rachel_Jackson" title="Rachel Jackson">Rachel Jackson</a> by <a href="/wiki/Ralph_Eleaser_Whiteside_Earl" title="Ralph Eleaser Whiteside Earl">Ralph Eleaser Whiteside Earl</a>, how housed at <a href="/wiki/The_Hermitage_(Nashville,_Tennessee)" title="The Hermitage (Nashville, Tennessee)">The Hermitage</a> in <a href="/wiki/Nashville,_Tennessee" title="Nashville, Tennessee">Nashville, Tennessee</a></figcaption></figure>
<p>After the <a href="/wiki/American_Revolutionary_War" title="American Revolutionary War">American Revolutionary War</a>, Jackson worked as a saddler,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERemini197727_23-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERemini197727-23">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup> briefly returned to school, and taught reading and writing to children.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBooraem2001133,_136_24-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBooraem2001133,_136-24">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup> In 1784, he left the Waxhaws region for <a href="/wiki/Salisbury,_North_Carolina" title="Salisbury, North Carolina">Salisbury, North Carolina</a>, where he <a href="/wiki/Reading_law" title="Reading law">studied law</a> under attorney Spruce Macay.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERemini197729_25-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERemini197729-25">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup> He completed his training under <a href="/wiki/John_Stokes_(North_Carolina_judge)" title="John Stokes (North Carolina judge)">John Stokes</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBrands200537_26-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrands200537-26">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup> and was admitted to the North Carolina bar in September 1787.<sup id="cite_ref-NC_State_Library_27-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-NC_State_Library-27">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup> Shortly thereafter, his friend <a href="/wiki/John_McNairy" title="John McNairy">John McNairy</a> helped him get appointed as a prosecuting attorney in the <a href="/wiki/Washington_District,_North_Carolina" title="Washington District, North Carolina">Western District</a> of North Carolina,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERemini197734_28-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERemini197734-28">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup> which would later become the state of Tennessee. While traveling to assume his new position, Jackson stopped in <a href="/wiki/Jonesborough,_Tennessee" title="Jonesborough, Tennessee">Jonesborough</a>. While there, he bought his first slave, a woman who was around his age.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERemini197737_29-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERemini197737-29">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup> He also fought his first duel, accusing another lawyer, <a href="/wiki/Waightstill_Avery" title="Waightstill Avery">Waightstill Avery</a>, of impugning his character. The duel ended with both men firing in the air.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBooraem2001190–191_30-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBooraem2001190–191-30">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Jackson began his new career in the frontier town of <a href="/wiki/Nashville,_Tennessee" title="Nashville, Tennessee">Nashville</a> in 1788 and quickly moved up in <a href="/wiki/Social_status" title="Social status">social status</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWilentz200518_31-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilentz200518-31">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup> He became a protégé of <a href="/wiki/William_Blount" title="William Blount">William Blount</a>, one of the most powerful men in the territory.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWilentz200519_32-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilentz200519-32">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup> Jackson was appointed attorney general in 1791 and judge advocate for the militia the following year.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERemini197753_33-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERemini197753-33">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup> He also got involved in land speculation,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERemini197787_34-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERemini197787-34">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup> eventually forming a partnership with fellow lawyer <a href="/wiki/John_Overton_(judge)" title="John Overton (judge)">John Overton</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEClifton195224_35-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEClifton195224-35">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup> Their partnership mainly dealt with claims made under a <a href="/wiki/Confederation_period#Western_settlement" title="Confederation period">'land grab' act of 1783</a> that opened <a href="/wiki/Cherokee" title="Cherokee">Cherokee</a> and <a href="/wiki/Chickasaw" title="Chickasaw">Chickasaw</a> territory to North Carolina's white residents.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDurham1990218–219_36-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDurham1990218–219-36">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>While boarding at the home of Rachel Stockly Donelson, the widow of <a href="/wiki/John_Donelson" title="John Donelson">John Donelson</a>, Jackson became acquainted with their daughter, <a href="/wiki/Rachel_Jackson" title="Rachel Jackson">Rachel Donelson Robards</a>. The younger Rachel was in an unhappy marriage with Captain Lewis Robards, and the two were separated by 1789.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEOwsley1977481–482_37-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEOwsley1977481–482-37">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup> After the separation, Jackson and Rachel became romantically involved,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBrands200563_38-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrands200563-38">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup> living together as husband and wife.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMeacham200822–23_39-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMeacham200822–23-39">&#91;38&#93;</a></sup> Robards petitioned for divorce, which was granted on the basis of Rachel's infidelity.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHowe2007277_40-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHowe2007277-40">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup> The couple legally married in January 1794.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBrands200565_41-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrands200565-41">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup> In 1796, they acquired their first plantation, Hunter's Hill,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERemini197768_42-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERemini197768-42">&#91;41&#93;</a></sup> on 640 acres (260&#160;ha) of land near Nashville.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBrands200573_43-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrands200573-43">&#91;42&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Early_public_career">Early public career</span></h3>
<p>Jackson became a member of the <a href="/wiki/Democratic-Republican_Party" title="Democratic-Republican Party">Democratic-Republican Party</a>, the dominant party in Tennessee.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWilentz200519_32-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilentz200519-32">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup> He was elected as a delegate to the Tennessee <a href="/wiki/Constituent_assembly" title="Constituent assembly">constitutional convention</a> in 1796.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWilentz200518–19_44-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilentz200518–19-44">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup> When Tennessee achieved statehood that year, he was elected to be its <a href="/wiki/United_States_House_of_Representatives" title="United States House of Representatives">U.S. representative</a>. In Congress, Jackson argued against the <a href="/wiki/Jay_Treaty" title="Jay Treaty">Jay Treaty</a>, criticized <a href="/wiki/George_Washington" title="George Washington">George Washington</a> for allegedly removing Democratic-Republicans from public office, and joined several other Democratic-Republican congressmen in voting against a resolution of thanks for Washington.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERemini197792–94_45-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERemini197792–94-45">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup> He advocated for the right of Tennesseans to militarily oppose Native American interests.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBrands200579–81_46-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrands200579–81-46">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup> The state legislature elected him to be a <a href="/wiki/United_States_Senate" title="United States Senate">U.S. senator</a> in 1797, but he resigned after serving only six months.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERemini1977112_47-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERemini1977112-47">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Upon returning to Tennessee, Jackson was elected as a judge of the <a href="/wiki/Tennessee_Supreme_Court" title="Tennessee Supreme Court">Tennessee superior court</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-US_Congress_Bio_48-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-US_Congress_Bio-48">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERemini1977113_49-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERemini1977113-49">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup>
In 1802, he also became major general, or commander, of the Tennessee <a href="/wiki/Militia_(United_States)" title="Militia (United States)">militia</a>, a position that was determined by a vote of the militia's officers. The vote was tied between Jackson and <a href="/wiki/John_Sevier" title="John Sevier">John Sevier</a>, a popular Revolutionary War veteran and former governor, but the current state governor, <a href="/wiki/Archibald_Roane" title="Archibald Roane">Archibald Roane</a>, broke the tie in Jackson's favor. Jackson later accused Sevier of fraud and bribery.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBrands2005104–105_50-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrands2005104–105-50">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup> Sevier responded by impugning Rachel's honor, resulting in a shootout on a public street.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMeacham200825_51-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMeacham200825-51">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup> Soon afterwards, they met to duel, but parted without having fired at each other.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERemini1977123_52-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERemini1977123-52">&#91;51&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Planting_career_and_slavery">Planting career and slavery</span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Aaron_and_Hannah_Jackson_(1865).jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/75/Aaron_and_Hannah_Jackson_%281865%29.jpg/220px-Aaron_and_Hannah_Jackson_%281865%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="299" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/75/Aaron_and_Hannah_Jackson_%281865%29.jpg/330px-Aaron_and_Hannah_Jackson_%281865%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/75/Aaron_and_Hannah_Jackson_%281865%29.jpg/440px-Aaron_and_Hannah_Jackson_%281865%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1200" data-file-height="1629" /></a><figcaption>Aaron and <a href="/wiki/Hannah_Jackson" title="Hannah Jackson">Hannah Jackson</a>, two slaves owned by Jackson, photographed by <a href="/wiki/T._M._Schleier" title="T. M. Schleier">Theodore Schleier</a> in 1865, now housed at The Hermitage in Nashville</figcaption></figure> 
<p>Jackson resigned his judgeship in 1804.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWilentz200521_53-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilentz200521-53">&#91;52&#93;</a></sup> He had almost gone bankrupt when the credit he used for land speculation collapsed in the wake of an <a href="/wiki/Panic_of_1796%E2%80%931797" title="Panic of 1796–1797">earlier financial panic</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESellers195476–77_54-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESellers195476–77-54">&#91;53&#93;</a></sup> He had to sell Hunters Hill, as well as 25,000 acres (10,000&#160;ha) of land he bought for speculation, and bought a smaller 420-acre (170&#160;ha) plantation near Nashville that he would call <a href="/wiki/The_Hermitage_(Nashville,_Tennessee)" title="The Hermitage (Nashville, Tennessee)">the Hermitage</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERemini1977131–132_55-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERemini1977131–132-55">&#91;54&#93;</a></sup> He focused on recovering from his losses by becoming a successful <a href="/wiki/Planter_class" title="Planter class">planter</a> and merchant.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERemini1977131–132_55-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERemini1977131–132-55">&#91;54&#93;</a></sup> The Hermitage would grow to 1,000 acres (400&#160;ha),<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERemini1977379_56-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERemini1977379-56">&#91;55&#93;</a></sup> making it one of the largest cotton-growing plantations in the state.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWilentz200521_53-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilentz200521-53">&#91;52&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Like most planters in the <a href="/wiki/Southern_United_States" title="Southern United States">Southern United States</a>, Jackson used <a href="/wiki/Slavery_in_the_United_States" title="Slavery in the United States">slave labor</a>. In 1804, Jackson had nine <a href="/wiki/African_Americans" title="African Americans">African American</a> slaves; by 1820, he had over 100; and by his death in 1845, he had over 150.<sup id="cite_ref-Hermitage_Slavery_2011_57-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Hermitage_Slavery_2011-57">&#91;56&#93;</a></sup> Over his lifetime, he owned a total of 300 slaves.<sup id="cite_ref-58" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-58">&#91;57&#93;</a></sup> Jackson subscribed to the <a href="/wiki/Paternalism" title="Paternalism">paternalistic</a> idea of slavery, which claimed that slave ownership was morally acceptable as long as slaves were treated with humanity and their basic needs were cared for.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWarshauer2006224_59-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWarshauer2006224-59">&#91;58&#93;</a></sup> In practice, slaves were treated as a form of wealth whose productivity needed to be protected.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECheathem2011328–329_60-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECheathem2011328–329-60">&#91;59&#93;</a></sup> Slaves who disobeyed or ran away could be harshly punished.<sup id="cite_ref-61" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-61">&#91;60&#93;</a></sup> For example, in an 1804 advertisement to recover a runaway slave, Jackson offered "ten dollars extra, for every hundred lashes any person will give him, to the amount of three hundred".<sup id="cite_ref-62" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-62">&#91;61&#93;</a></sup> Jackson also participated in the local slave trade.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERemini197755_63-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERemini197755-63">&#91;62&#93;</a></sup> Over time, his accumulation of wealth in both slaves and land placed him among the elite families of Tennessee.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMeacham200835_64-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMeacham200835-64">&#91;63&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Duel_with_Dickinson_and_adventure_with_Burr">Duel with Dickinson and adventure with Burr</span></h3>
<p>In May 1806, Jackson fought a duel with <a href="/wiki/Charles_Dickinson_(historical_figure)" title="Charles Dickinson (historical figure)">Charles Dickinson</a>. They had gotten into an argument over a horse race, and Dickinson allegedly uttered a slur against Rachel.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMeacham200825_51-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMeacham200825-51">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup> During the duel, Dickinson fired first, and the bullet hit Jackson in the chest. The wound was not life-threatening because the bullet had shattered against his breastbone.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBrands2005138_65-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrands2005138-65">&#91;64&#93;</a></sup> Jackson returned fire and killed Dickinson. The killing tarnished Jackson's reputation.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERemini1977143_66-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERemini1977143-66">&#91;65&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Later in the year, Jackson became involved in former vice president <a href="/wiki/Aaron_Burr" title="Aaron Burr">Aaron Burr</a>'s <a href="/wiki/Burr_conspiracy" title="Burr conspiracy">plan</a> to conquer <a href="/wiki/Spanish_Florida" title="Spanish Florida">Spanish Florida</a> and drive the Spanish from Texas. Jackson had first gotten to know Burr in 1805 when he stayed with the Jacksons at the Hermitage during a tour of what was then the Western United States that he had embarked on after mortally wounding <a href="/wiki/Alexander_Hamilton" title="Alexander Hamilton">Alexander Hamilton</a> in the <a href="/wiki/Burr%E2%80%93Hamilton_duel" title="Burr–Hamilton duel">Burr–Hamilton duel</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMeacham200827_67-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMeacham200827-67">&#91;66&#93;</a></sup> Burr eventually persuaded Jackson to join his adventure. In October 1806, Jackson wrote <a href="/wiki/James_Winchester_(general)" title="James Winchester (general)">James Winchester</a> that the United States "can conquer not only the Floridas [at that time there was an <a href="/wiki/East_Florida" title="East Florida">East Florida</a> and a <a href="/wiki/West_Florida" title="West Florida">West Florida</a>], but all Spanish North America".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERemini1977149_68-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERemini1977149-68">&#91;67&#93;</a></sup> He informed the Tennessee militia that it should be ready to march at a moment's notice "when the government and constituted authority of our country require it",<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERemini1977148_69-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERemini1977148-69">&#91;68&#93;</a></sup> and agreed to provide boats and provisions for the expedition.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMeacham200827_67-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMeacham200827-67">&#91;66&#93;</a></sup> Jackson sent a letter to president <a href="/wiki/Thomas_Jefferson" title="Thomas Jefferson">Thomas Jefferson</a> telling him that Tennessee was ready to defend the nation's honor.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBrands2005120_70-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrands2005120-70">&#91;69&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Jackson also expressed uncertainty about the enterprise. He warned the Governor of Louisiana <a href="/wiki/William_C._C._Claiborne" title="William C. C. Claiborne">William Claiborne</a> and Tennessee Senator <a href="/wiki/Daniel_Smith_(surveyor)" title="Daniel Smith (surveyor)">Daniel Smith</a> that some of the people involved in the adventure might be intending to break away from the United States.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERemini1977151_71-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERemini1977151-71">&#91;70&#93;</a></sup> In December, Jefferson ordered Burr to be arrested for treason.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMeacham200827_67-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMeacham200827-67">&#91;66&#93;</a></sup> Jackson, safe from arrest because of his extensive paper trail, organized the militia to capture the conspirators.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERemini1977153_72-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERemini1977153-72">&#91;71&#93;</a></sup> Jackson testified before a grand jury at Burr's trial in 1807, implying that it was Burr's associate <a href="/wiki/James_Wilkinson" title="James Wilkinson">James Wilkinson</a> who was guilty of treason, not Burr. Burr was acquitted of the charges.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBrands2005127–128_73-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrands2005127–128-73">&#91;72&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Military_career">Military career</span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1066479718"><table class="infobox vcard" style="border-spacing:2px;"><tbody><tr><th colspan="2" class="infobox-above" style="font-size:125%; background-color:#C3D6EF;"><div style="display:inline" class="fn">Military campaigns<br /> of Andrew Jackson</div></th></tr><tr><td colspan="2" class="infobox-image" style="border-bottom: 1px solid #aaa;; line-height: 1.5em"><span class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Frameless"><a href="/wiki/File:General_Andrew_Jackson_MET_DT2851.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="Gray-haired man in army uniform with epaulettes" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c3/General_Andrew_Jackson_MET_DT2851.jpg/220px-General_Andrew_Jackson_MET_DT2851.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="293" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c3/General_Andrew_Jackson_MET_DT2851.jpg/330px-General_Andrew_Jackson_MET_DT2851.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c3/General_Andrew_Jackson_MET_DT2851.jpg/440px-General_Andrew_Jackson_MET_DT2851.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2796" data-file-height="3722" /></a></span><div class="infobox-caption" style="font-size: 100%"><i>General Andrew Jackson</i> a circa 1819 portrait by <a href="/wiki/John_Wesley_Jarvis" title="John Wesley Jarvis">John Wesley Jarvis</a> now housed at <a href="/wiki/Metropolitan_Museum_of_Art" title="Metropolitan Museum of Art">Metropolitan Museum of Art</a> in <a href="/wiki/New_York_City" title="New York City">New York City</a></div></td></tr><tr><td colspan="2" class="infobox-full-data"><div class="center"><div class="thumb tnone">
<div class="thumbinner" style="width:212px">
<div class="thumbcaption" style="font-size:75%">

<div class="thumbcaption"><style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r981673959">.mw-parser-output .legend{page-break-inside:avoid;break-inside:avoid-column}.mw-parser-output .legend-color{display:inline-block;min-width:1.25em;height:1.25em;line-height:1.25;margin:1px 0;text-align:center;border:1px solid black;background-color:transparent;color:black}.mw-parser-output .legend-text{}</style><div class="legend"><span class="legend-color mw-no-invert" style="background-color:#117733; color:white;">&#160;</span>&#160;<a href="/wiki/Creek_War" title="Creek War">Creek War</a></div><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r981673959"><div class="legend"><span class="legend-color mw-no-invert" style="background-color:#882255; color:white;">&#160;</span>&#160;<a href="/wiki/War_of_1812" title="War of 1812">War of 1812</a></div><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r981673959"><div class="legend"><span class="legend-color mw-no-invert" style="background-color:#999933; color:black;">&#160;</span>&#160;<a href="/wiki/Seminole_Wars#First_Seminole_War" title="Seminole Wars">First Seminole War</a></div></div>
</div></div></div></td></tr></tbody></table>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="War_of_1812">War of 1812</span></h3>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Creek_War">Creek War</span></h4>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Creek_War" title="Creek War">Creek War</a></div>
<p>On June 18, 1812, the United States declared war on the <a href="/wiki/United_Kingdom_of_Great_Britain_and_Ireland" title="United Kingdom of Great Britain and Ireland">United Kingdom</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHickey198946_74-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHickey198946-74">&#91;73&#93;</a></sup> Though the <a href="/wiki/Origins_of_the_War_of_1812" title="Origins of the War of 1812">causes</a> of the <a href="/wiki/War_of_1812" title="War of 1812">War of 1812</a> were primarily about maritime issues,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHickey198972_75-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHickey198972-75">&#91;74&#93;</a></sup> the war provided the white settlers on the southern frontier the opportunity to overcome Native American resistance to settlement, undermine British support of the Native American tribes,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBrands2005175_76-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrands2005175-76">&#91;75&#93;</a></sup> and pry Florida from the Spanish.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERemini1977166_77-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERemini1977166-77">&#91;76&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Jackson immediately offered to raise volunteers for the war, but he was not called to duty until after the United States military was repeatedly defeated in the <a href="/wiki/War_of_1812#Invasions_of_Canada,_1812" title="War of 1812">American Northwest</a>. After these defeats, in January 1813, Jackson enlisted over 2,000 volunteers,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERemini1977173_78-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERemini1977173-78">&#91;77&#93;</a></sup> who were ordered to head to <a href="/wiki/New_Orleans" title="New Orleans">New Orleans</a> to defend against a British attack.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBrands2005179_79-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrands2005179-79">&#91;78&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-80" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-80">&#91;79&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWilentz200523–25_81-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilentz200523–25-81">&#91;80&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-82" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-82">&#91;81&#93;</a></sup> When his forces arrived at <a href="/wiki/Natchez,_Mississippi" title="Natchez, Mississippi">Natchez</a>, they were ordered to halt by General Wilkinson, the commander at New Orleans and the man Jackson accused of treason after the Burr adventure. A little later, Jackson received a letter from the <a href="/wiki/United_States_Secretary_of_War" title="United States Secretary of War">Secretary of War</a>, <a href="/wiki/John_Armstrong_Jr." title="John Armstrong Jr.">John Armstrong</a>, stating that his volunteers were not needed,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWilentz200522–23_16-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilentz200522–23-16">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup> and that they were to be disbanded and any supplies were to be handed over to Wilkinson.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBrands2005184_83-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrands2005184-83">&#91;82&#93;</a></sup> Jackson refused to disband his troops; instead, he led them on the difficult march back to Nashville, earning the nickname "<a href="/wiki/Hickory" title="Hickory">Hickory</a>" (later "Old Hickory") for his toughness.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMeacham200823_84-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMeacham200823-84">&#91;83&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>After returning to Nashville, Jackson and one of his colonels, <a href="/wiki/John_Coffee" title="John Coffee">John Coffee</a>, got into a street brawl over honor with the brothers Jesse and <a href="/wiki/Thomas_Hart_Benton_(politician)" title="Thomas Hart Benton (politician)">Thomas Hart Benton</a>. Nobody was killed, but Jackson received a gunshot in the shoulder that nearly killed him.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWilentz200523_85-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilentz200523-85">&#91;84&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Jackson had not fully recovered from his wounds when Governor Blount called out the militia in September 1813 following the August <a href="/wiki/Fort_Mims_Massacre" class="mw-redirect" title="Fort Mims Massacre">Fort Mims Massacre</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEOwsley198161–62_86-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEOwsley198161–62-86">&#91;85&#93;</a></sup> The <a href="/wiki/Red_Sticks" title="Red Sticks">Red Sticks</a>, a confederate faction that had allied with <a href="/wiki/Tecumseh" title="Tecumseh">Tecumseh</a>, a <a href="/wiki/Shawnee" title="Shawnee">Shawnee</a> chief who was fighting with the British against the United States, killed about 250 militia men and civilians at Fort Mims in retaliation for an ambush by American militia at <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Burnt_Corn" title="Battle of Burnt Corn">Burnt Corn Creek</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDavis2002631–632_87-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDavis2002631–632-87">&#91;86&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEOwsley198138–39_88-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEOwsley198138–39-88">&#91;87&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Jackson's objective was to destroy the Red Sticks.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEOwsley198140_89-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEOwsley198140-89">&#91;88&#93;</a></sup> He headed south from <a href="/wiki/Fayetteville,_Tennessee" title="Fayetteville, Tennessee">Fayetteville, Tennessee</a> in October with 2,500 militia, establishing <a href="/wiki/Fort_Strother" title="Fort Strother">Fort Strother</a> as his supply base.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERemini1977192–193_90-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERemini1977192–193-90">&#91;89&#93;</a></sup> He sent his cavalry under General Coffee ahead of the main force, destroying Red Stick villages and capturing supplies.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBrands2005197_91-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrands2005197-91">&#91;90&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEOwsley198163–64_92-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEOwsley198163–64-92">&#91;91&#93;</a></sup> On November 3, Coffee defeated a band of Red Sticks at the <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Tallushatchee" title="Battle of Tallushatchee">Battle of Tallushatchee</a>. Later in the month, Jackson defeated another band of Red Sticks who were besieging Creek allies at the <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Talladega" title="Battle of Talladega">Battle of Talladega</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERemini1977196–197_93-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERemini1977196–197-93">&#91;92&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>By January 1814, the expiration of enlistments and desertion had reduced Jackson's force by about 1,000 volunteers,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEOwsley198172–73_94-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEOwsley198172–73-94">&#91;93&#93;</a></sup> but Jackson continued the offensive.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKanon19994_95-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKanon19994-95">&#91;94&#93;</a></sup> The Red Sticks counterattacked at the <a href="/wiki/Battles_of_Emuckfaw_and_Enotachopo_Creek" title="Battles of Emuckfaw and Enotachopo Creek">Battles of Emuckfaw and Enotachopo Creek</a>. Jackson repelled them, but he was forced to withdraw to Fort Strother.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEOwsley198175–76_96-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEOwsley198175–76-96">&#91;95&#93;</a></sup> Jackson's army was reinforced by further recruitment and the addition of regular army unit, the <a href="/wiki/39th_Infantry_Regiment_(War_of_1812)" title="39th Infantry Regiment (War of 1812)">39th U. S. Infantry Regiment</a> commanded by Colonel <a href="/wiki/John_Williams_(Tennessee_politician)" title="John Williams (Tennessee politician)">John Williams</a>. The combined force of 3000 men—including Cherokee, Choctaw, and Creek allies–attacked a fort the Red Sticks had built at <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Horseshoe_Bend" title="Battle of Horseshoe Bend">Horseshoe Bend</a> on the <a href="/wiki/Tallapoosa_River" title="Tallapoosa River">Tallapoosa River</a>. Jackson's forces numbered over 3,000 men; the Red Sticks had about 1,000.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEOwsley198179_97-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEOwsley198179-97">&#91;96&#93;</a></sup> The Red Sticks were overwhelmed and massacred.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKanon19994–10_98-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKanon19994–10-98">&#91;97&#93;</a></sup> Almost all their warriors were killed, and nearly 300 women and children were taken prisoner and distributed to Jackson's Native American allies.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKanon19994–10_98-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKanon19994–10-98">&#91;97&#93;</a></sup> The victory broke the power of the Red Sticks.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEOwsley198181_99-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEOwsley198181-99">&#91;98&#93;</a></sup> Jackson continued his <a href="/wiki/Scorched-earth" class="mw-redirect" title="Scorched-earth">scorched-earth</a> campaign of burning villages, destroying supplies,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEOwsley198181_99-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEOwsley198181-99">&#91;98&#93;</a></sup> and starving Red Stick women and children.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBrands2005220_100-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrands2005220-100">&#91;99&#93;</a></sup> The campaign ended when <a href="/wiki/William_Weatherford" title="William Weatherford">William Weatherford</a>, the Red Stick leader, surrendered,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWilentz200527_101-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilentz200527-101">&#91;100&#93;</a></sup> although some Red Sticks fled to East Florida.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEOwsley198187_102-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEOwsley198187-102">&#91;101&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On June 8, Jackson was appointed a <a href="/wiki/Brigadier_general_(United_States)" title="Brigadier general (United States)">brigadier general</a> in the <a href="/wiki/United_States_Army" title="United States Army">United States Army</a>, and 10 days later was made a <a href="/wiki/Brevet_(military)" title="Brevet (military)">brevet</a> <a href="/wiki/Major_general_(United_States)" title="Major general (United States)">major general</a> with command of the Seventh Military District, which included Tennessee, Louisiana, the Mississippi Territory, and the Muscogee Creek Confederacy.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERemini1977222_103-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERemini1977222-103">&#91;102&#93;</a></sup> With President <a href="/wiki/James_Madison" title="James Madison">James Madison</a>'s approval, Jackson imposed the <a href="/wiki/Treaty_of_Fort_Jackson" title="Treaty of Fort Jackson">Treaty of Fort Jackson</a>. The treaty required all Creek, including those who had remained allies, to surrender 23,000,000 acres (9,300,000&#160;ha) of land to the United States.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWilentz200527–28_104-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilentz200527–28-104">&#91;103&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Jackson then turned his attention to the British and Spanish. He moved his forces to <a href="/wiki/Mobile,_Alabama" title="Mobile, Alabama">Mobile, Alabama</a> in August. He accused the Spanish governor of West Florida, <a href="/wiki/Mateo_Gonz%C3%A1lez_Manrique" title="Mateo González Manrique">Mateo González Manrique</a>, of arming the Red Sticks and threatened to attack. The governor responded by inviting the British to land at Pensacola to defend it, which violated Spanish neutrality.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERemini1977236–237_105-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERemini1977236–237-105">&#91;104&#93;</a></sup> The British attempted to capture Mobile, but their invasion fleet was repulsed at <a href="/wiki/Fort_Bowyer#First_battle" title="Fort Bowyer">Fort Bowyer</a>, located at the mouth of <a href="/wiki/Mobile_Bay" title="Mobile Bay">Mobile Bay</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERemini1977238_106-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERemini1977238-106">&#91;105&#93;</a></sup> Jackson then invaded Florida, defeating the Spanish and British forces at the <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Pensacola_(1814)" title="Battle of Pensacola (1814)">Battle of Pensacola</a> on November 7.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEOwsley1981116–117_107-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEOwsley1981116–117-107">&#91;106&#93;</a></sup> Afterwards, the Spanish surrendered and the British withdrew. Weeks later, Jackson learned that the British were planning an attack on New Orleans, which was the gateway to the <a href="/wiki/Lower_Mississippi_River" title="Lower Mississippi River">Lower Mississippi River</a> and control of the American West.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWilentz200528_108-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilentz200528-108">&#91;107&#93;</a></sup> He evacuated Pensacola, strengthened the garrison at Mobile,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEOwsley1981118_109-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEOwsley1981118-109">&#91;108&#93;</a></sup> and led his troops to New Orleans.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERemini1977244–245_110-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERemini1977244–245-110">&#91;109&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Battle_of_New_Orleans">Battle of New Orleans</span></h4>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_New_Orleans" title="Battle of New Orleans">Battle of New Orleans</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Battle_of_New_Orleans.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="Blue U.S. soldiers stand behind an earthen wall as red-coated British soldiers charge. Jackson stands atop the parapet with his right hand outstretched and holding a sword." src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/35/Battle_of_New_Orleans.jpg/220px-Battle_of_New_Orleans.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="163" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/35/Battle_of_New_Orleans.jpg/330px-Battle_of_New_Orleans.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/35/Battle_of_New_Orleans.jpg/440px-Battle_of_New_Orleans.jpg 2x" data-file-width="3488" data-file-height="2579" /></a><figcaption><i>The Battle of New Orleans</i>, a 1910 portrait by <a href="/wiki/Edward_Percy_Moran" title="Edward Percy Moran">Edward Percy Moran</a> depicting the <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_New_Orleans" title="Battle of New Orleans">Battle of New Orleans</a></figcaption></figure>
<p>Jackson arrived in New Orleans on December 1, 1814.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERemini1977247_111-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERemini1977247-111">&#91;110&#93;</a></sup> There he instituted <a href="/wiki/Martial_law" title="Martial law">martial law</a> because he worried about the loyalty of the city's <a href="/wiki/Louisiana_Creole_people" title="Louisiana Creole people">Creole</a> and Spanish inhabitants. He augmented his force by forming an alliance with <a href="/wiki/Jean_Lafitte" title="Jean Lafitte">Jean Lafitte</a>'s smugglers and raising units of free African Americans and Creek,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWilentz200529_112-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilentz200529-112">&#91;111&#93;</a></sup> paying non-white volunteers the same salary as whites.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERemini1977254_113-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERemini1977254-113">&#91;112&#93;</a></sup> This gave Jackson a force of about 5,000 men when the British arrived.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERemini1977274_114-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERemini1977274-114">&#91;113&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The British arrived in New Orleans in mid-December.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEOwsley1981138_115-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEOwsley1981138-115">&#91;114&#93;</a></sup> Admiral Cochrane was the overall commander of the operation;<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEOwsley1981134,_136_116-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEOwsley1981134,_136-116">&#91;115&#93;</a></sup> General <a href="/wiki/Edward_Pakenham" title="Edward Pakenham">Edward Pakenham</a> commanded the army of 10,000 soldiers, many of whom had served in the Napoleonic Wars.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWilentz200529–30_117-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilentz200529–30-117">&#91;116&#93;</a></sup> As the British advanced up the east bank of the Mississippi River, Jackson constructed a fortified position to block them.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERemini1977268–269_118-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERemini1977268–269-118">&#91;117&#93;</a></sup> The <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_New_Orleans" title="Battle of New Orleans">climactic battle</a> took place on January 8 when the British launched a frontal assault. Their troops made easy targets for the Americans protected by their parapets, and the attack ended in disaster.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWilentz200531–32_119-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilentz200531–32-119">&#91;118&#93;</a></sup> General Pakenham was killed and the British suffered over 2,000 casualties; the Americans had suffered about 60 casualties.<sup id="cite_ref-Battle_of_New_Orleans_120-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Battle_of_New_Orleans-120">&#91;119&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The British decamped from New Orleans at the end of January, but they still remained a threat.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEOwsley1981169_121-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEOwsley1981169-121">&#91;120&#93;</a></sup> Jackson refused to lift martial law and kept the militia under arms. He approved the execution of six militiamen for desertion.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETregle1981337_122-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETregle1981337-122">&#91;121&#93;</a></sup> Some Creoles registered as French citizens with the French consul and demanded to be discharged from the militia due to their foreign nationality. Jackson then ordered all French citizens to leave the city within three days,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERemini1977309_123-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERemini1977309-123">&#91;122&#93;</a></sup> and had a member of the Louisiana legislature, Louis Louaillier, arrested when he wrote a newspaper article criticizing Jackson's continuation of martial law. U.S. District Court Judge <a href="/wiki/Dominic_Augustin_Hall" title="Dominic Augustin Hall">Dominic A. Hall</a> signed a writ of <i><a href="/wiki/Habeas_corpus" title="Habeas corpus">habeas corpus</a></i> for Louailler's release. Jackson had <a href="/wiki/Arrest_of_Dominic_Hall_and_Louis_Louaillier" title="Arrest of Dominic Hall and Louis Louaillier">Hall arrested</a> too. A military court ordered Louiallier's release, but Jackson kept him in prison and evicted Hall from the city.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETregle1981377–378_124-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETregle1981377–378-124">&#91;123&#93;</a></sup> Although Jackson lifted martial law when he received official word that the <a href="/wiki/Treaty_of_Ghent" title="Treaty of Ghent">Treaty of Ghent</a>, which ended the war with the British, had been signed,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERemini1977312_125-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERemini1977312-125">&#91;124&#93;</a></sup> his previous behavior tainted his reputation in New Orleans.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETregle1981378–379_126-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETregle1981378–379-126">&#91;125&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Jackson's victory made him a national hero,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWilentz200529–33_127-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilentz200529–33-127">&#91;126&#93;</a></sup> and on February 27, 1815, he was given the <a href="/wiki/Thanks_of_Congress" title="Thanks of Congress">Thanks of Congress</a> and awarded a <a href="/wiki/Congressional_Gold_Medal" title="Congressional Gold Medal">Congressional Gold Medal</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-US_Congress_Bio_48-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-US_Congress_Bio-48">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup> Though the Treaty of Ghent had been signed in December 1814 before the Battle of New Orleans was fought,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMeacham200832_128-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMeacham200832-128">&#91;127&#93;</a></sup> Jackson's victory assured that the United States control of the region between Mobile and New Orleans would not be effectively contested by European powers. This control allowed the American government to ignore one of the articles in the treaty, which would have returned the Creek lands taken in the Treaty of Fort Jackson.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEOwsley1981178–179_129-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEOwsley1981178–179-129">&#91;128&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="First_Seminole_War">First Seminole War</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Seminole_Wars#First_Seminole_War" title="Seminole Wars">Seminole Wars §&#160;First Seminole War</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Ambristertrial.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="Two soldiers stand trial. Several other men gather around." src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/54/Ambristertrial.jpg/220px-Ambristertrial.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="173" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/54/Ambristertrial.jpg/330px-Ambristertrial.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/54/Ambristertrial.jpg/440px-Ambristertrial.jpg 2x" data-file-width="600" data-file-height="472" /></a><figcaption>A circa 1846 engraving of the trial of <a href="/wiki/Arbuthnot_and_Ambrister_incident" title="Arbuthnot and Ambrister incident">Robert Ambrister</a> by <a href="/wiki/William_Croome" title="William Croome">William Croome</a> in John Frost's <i>Pictorial Life of Andrew Jackson</i></figcaption></figure>
<p>Following the war, Jackson remained in command of troops in the southern half of the United States and was permitted to make his headquarters at the Hermitage.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERemini1977321_130-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERemini1977321-130">&#91;129&#93;</a></sup> Jackson continued to displace the Native Americans in areas under his command. Despite resistance from the Secretary of the Treasury <a href="/wiki/William_H._Crawford" title="William H. Crawford">William Crawford</a>, who tried to help the Native Americans retain their land, Jackson signed five treaties between 1816 and 1820, including the <a href="/wiki/Treaty_of_Tuscaloosa" title="Treaty of Tuscaloosa">Treaty of Tuscaloosa</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Treaty_of_Doak%27s_Stand" title="Treaty of Doak&#39;s Stand">Treaty of Doak's Stand</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEClarkGuice1996233–243_131-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEClarkGuice1996233–243-131">&#91;130&#93;</a></sup> in which the Creek, Choctaw, Cherokee and Chickasaw ceded tens of millions of acres of land to the United States.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWilentz200536_132-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilentz200536-132">&#91;131&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Jackson would soon find himself embroiled in conflict in the Floridas. The former British post at <a href="/wiki/Negro_Fort" title="Negro Fort">Prospect Bluff</a>, which became known to Americans as "the Negro fort", remained occupied by more than a thousand former soldiers of the British Royal and Colonial Marines, escaped <a href="/wiki/Runaway_slaves" class="mw-redirect" title="Runaway slaves">slaves</a>, and various indigenous peoples.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWright1968569_133-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWright1968569-133">&#91;132&#93;</a></sup> It had become a magnet for escapees<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWright1968569_133-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWright1968569-133">&#91;132&#93;</a></sup> and was seen as a threat to the property rights of American enslavers,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPorter1951261–262_134-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPorter1951261–262-134">&#91;133&#93;</a></sup> even a potential source of insurrection by enslaved people.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMissallMissall200426_135-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMissallMissall200426-135">&#91;134&#93;</a></sup>
Jackson ordered Colonel <a href="/wiki/Duncan_Lamont_Clinch" title="Duncan Lamont Clinch">Duncan Clinch</a> to capture the fort in July 1816. He destroyed it and killed many of the garrison. Some survivors were enslaved while others fled into the wilderness of Florida.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMissallMissall200428–30_136-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMissallMissall200428–30-136">&#91;135&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In addition, White settlers were in constant conflict with Native American people collectively known as the <a href="/wiki/Seminole" title="Seminole">Seminoles</a>, who straddled the border between the U.S. and Florida.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMissallMissall200432–33_137-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMissallMissall200432–33-137">&#91;136&#93;</a></sup> In December 1817, Secretary of War <a href="/wiki/John_C._Calhoun" title="John C. Calhoun">John C. Calhoun</a> initiated what would be called the <a href="/wiki/Seminole_Wars#First_Seminole_War" title="Seminole Wars">First Seminole War</a> by ordering Jackson to lead a campaign "with full power to conduct the war as he may think best".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMahon199864&#93;_138-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMahon199864]-138">&#91;137&#93;</a></sup> Jackson believed the best way to do this was to seize Florida from Spain once and for all. Before departing, Jackson wrote to President <a href="/wiki/James_Monroe" title="James Monroe">James Monroe</a>, "Let it be signified to me through any channel&#160;... that the possession of the Floridas would be desirable to the United States, and in sixty days it will be accomplished."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEOgg191966_139-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEOgg191966-139">&#91;138&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Jackson invaded Florida, captured the Spanish fort of <a href="/wiki/San_Marcos_de_Apalache_Historic_State_Park#History" title="San Marcos de Apalache Historic State Park">St. Marks</a>, and occupied Pensacola. Seminole and Spanish resistance was effectively ended by May 1818. He also captured two British agents, <a href="/wiki/Arbuthnot_and_Ambrister_incident" title="Arbuthnot and Ambrister incident">Robert Ambrister and Alexander Arbuthnot</a>, who had been working with the Seminoles. After a brief trial, Jackson executed both of them, causing a diplomatic incident with the British. Jackson's actions polarized Monroe's cabinet. The occupied territories were returned to Spain.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMahon199865–67_140-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMahon199865–67-140">&#91;139&#93;</a></sup> Calhoun wanted him censured for violating the Constitution, since the United States had not <a href="/wiki/Declaration_of_war_by_the_United_States" title="Declaration of war by the United States">declared war</a> on Spain. <a href="/wiki/United_States_Secretary_of_State" title="United States Secretary of State">Secretary of State</a> <a href="/wiki/John_Quincy_Adams" title="John Quincy Adams">John Quincy Adams</a> defended him as he thought Jackson's occupation of Pensacola would lead Spain to sell Florida, which Spain did in the <a href="/wiki/Adams%E2%80%93On%C3%ADs_Treaty" title="Adams–Onís Treaty">Adams–Onís Treaty</a> of 1819.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWilentz200538–39_141-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilentz200538–39-141">&#91;140&#93;</a></sup> In February 1819, a congressional investigation exonerated Jackson, <sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHeidler1993518_142-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHeidler1993518-142">&#91;141&#93;</a></sup> and his victory was instrumental in convincing the Seminoles to sign <a href="/wiki/Treaty_of_Moultrie_Creek" title="Treaty of Moultrie Creek">Treaty of Moultrie Creek</a> in 1823, which surrendered much of their land in Florida.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMahon1962350–354_143-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMahon1962350–354-143">&#91;142&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Presidential_aspirations">Presidential aspirations</span></h2>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Election_of_1824">Election of 1824</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/1824_United_States_presidential_election" title="1824 United States presidential election">1824 United States presidential election</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Andrew_Jackson.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="A man with wavy gray hair in white shirt, black bowtie, and black coat. Faces left." src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/64/Andrew_Jackson.jpg/220px-Andrew_Jackson.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="267" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/64/Andrew_Jackson.jpg/330px-Andrew_Jackson.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/64/Andrew_Jackson.jpg/440px-Andrew_Jackson.jpg 2x" data-file-width="610" data-file-height="739" /></a><figcaption>An 1824 portrait of Jackson by <a href="/wiki/Thomas_Sully" title="Thomas Sully">Thomas Sully</a></figcaption></figure>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Electoral_Votes_for_1824-_Focus_on_Jackson.png" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="Refer to caption" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f1/Electoral_Votes_for_1824-_Focus_on_Jackson.png/220px-Electoral_Votes_for_1824-_Focus_on_Jackson.png" decoding="async" width="220" height="226" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f1/Electoral_Votes_for_1824-_Focus_on_Jackson.png/330px-Electoral_Votes_for_1824-_Focus_on_Jackson.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f1/Electoral_Votes_for_1824-_Focus_on_Jackson.png/440px-Electoral_Votes_for_1824-_Focus_on_Jackson.png 2x" data-file-width="1993" data-file-height="2048" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/1824_United_States_presidential_election" title="1824 United States presidential election">1824 U.S. presidential election</a> results in which Jackson received a plurality of Electoral College votes. Subsequently <a href="/wiki/John_Quincy_Adams" title="John Quincy Adams">John Quincy Adams</a> was elected the seventh <a href="/wiki/President_of_the_United_States" title="President of the United States">president of the United States</a>.</figcaption></figure>
<p>In 1819, mismanagement by the <a href="/wiki/Second_Bank_of_the_United_States" title="Second Bank of the United States">Second Bank of the United States</a> created a <a href="/wiki/Panic_of_1819" title="Panic of 1819">financial panic</a> that sent the U.S. into its first prolonged financial depression. The United States reduced its military and Jackson was forced to retire from his major general position.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWilentz200540_144-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilentz200540-144">&#91;143&#93;</a></sup> In compensation, Monroe made him the first territorial governor of Florida in 1821.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBrands2005356–357_145-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrands2005356–357-145">&#91;144&#93;</a></sup> Jackson served as the governor for two months, returning to the Hermitage in ill health.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERemini19812_146-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERemini19812-146">&#91;145&#93;</a></sup> During his convalescence, Jackson, who had been a <a href="/wiki/Freemasonry" title="Freemasonry">Freemason</a> since at least 1798, became the <a href="/wiki/Grand_Master_(Masonic)" class="mw-redirect" title="Grand Master (Masonic)">Grand Master</a> of the <a href="/wiki/Grand_Lodge_of_Tennessee" title="Grand Lodge of Tennessee">Grand Lodge of Tennessee</a> for 1822–1823.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBurstein200339_147-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBurstein200339-147">&#91;146&#93;</a></sup> Around this time, he also completed negotiations for Tennessee to purchase Chickasaw lands. This became known as the <a href="/wiki/Jackson_Purchase_(U.S._historical_region)" title="Jackson Purchase (U.S. historical region)">Jackson Purchase</a>. Jackson, Overton, and another colleague had speculated in some of the land and used their portion to found the town of <a href="/wiki/Memphis,_Tennessee" title="Memphis, Tennessee">Memphis</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Jackson_Purchase_148-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Jackson_Purchase-148">&#91;147&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1822, Jackson accepted a plan by Overton to nominate him as a candidate for the 1824 presidential election, and he was nominated by the Tennessee legislature in July.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERemini198148–49_149-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERemini198148–49-149">&#91;148&#93;</a></sup> At the time, the Federalist Party had collapsed, and there were four major contenders for the Democratic-Republican Party nomination: William Crawford, John Quincy Adams, Henry Clay and John C. Calhoun. Jackson was intended to be a <a href="/wiki/Stalking_horse" title="Stalking horse">stalking horse</a> candidate to prevent Tennessee's electoral votes from going to Crawford, who was seen as a Washington insider. Unexpectedly, Jackson garnered popular support outside of Tennessee and became a serious candidate.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWilentz200540_144-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilentz200540-144">&#91;143&#93;</a></sup> He benefited from the expansion of suffrage among white males that followed the conclusion of the War of 1812.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESchlesinger194536–38_150-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESchlesinger194536–38-150">&#91;149&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHowe2007489–492_151-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHowe2007489–492-151">&#91;150&#93;</a></sup> He was a popular war hero whose reputation suggested he had the decisiveness and independence to bring change to how the government was run.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPhillips1976501_152-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPhillips1976501-152">&#91;151&#93;</a></sup> He also was promoted as a Washington outsider who stood for all the people, blaming banks for the country's depression.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWilentz200541–42,_45–46_153-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilentz200541–42,_45–46-153">&#91;152&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>During his presidential candidacy, Jackson reluctantly ran for one of Tennessee's U.S. Senate seats. <a href="/wiki/William_Berkeley_Lewis" title="William Berkeley Lewis">William Berkeley Lewis</a> and the other U.S. senator <a href="/wiki/John_Eaton_(politician)" title="John Eaton (politician)">John Eaton</a>, who were Jackson's political managers, convinced him that he needed to defeat incumbent John Williams, who openly opposed Jackson. The legislature elected him in October 1823.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERemini198151–52_154-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERemini198151–52-154">&#91;153&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBrands2005376–377_155-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrands2005376–377-155">&#91;154&#93;</a></sup> Jackson was attentive to his senatorial duties. He was appointed chairman of the <a href="/wiki/United_States_Senate_Committee_on_Armed_Services" title="United States Senate Committee on Armed Services">Committee on Military Affairs</a>, but avoided debate or initiating legislation.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERemini198167_156-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERemini198167-156">&#91;155&#93;</a></sup> He used his time in the Senate to form alliances and make peace with old adversaries.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMeacham200838_157-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMeacham200838-157">&#91;156&#93;</a></sup> Eaton continued to campaign for Jackson's presidency, updating his biography, and writing letters under a pseudonym that were quoted in newspapers around the country and so popular that they were reprinted in pamphlet form—Jackson himself had a part in their composition.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERemini198175–77_158-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERemini198175–77-158">&#91;157&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Democratic-Republican presidential nominees had historically been chosen by informal <a href="/wiki/Congressional_nominating_caucus" title="Congressional nominating caucus">congressional nominating caucuses</a>.
