In 1824, most of the Democratic-Republicans in Congress boycotted the caucus,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMorgan1969195_159-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMorgan1969195-159">&#91;158&#93;</a></sup> and the power to choose nominees was shifting to state nominating committees and legislatures.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWilentz200545_160-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilentz200545-160">&#91;159&#93;</a></sup> Jackson was nominated by a Pennsylvania convention, making him not merely a regional candidate from the west but the leading national contender.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPhillips1976490_161-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPhillips1976490-161">&#91;160&#93;</a></sup> When Jackson won the Pennsylvania nomination, Calhoun dropped out of the presidential race.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENiven1988101_162-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENiven1988101-162">&#91;161&#93;</a></sup> Afterwards, Jackson won the nomination in six other states and had a strong second-place finish in three others.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWilentz200546_163-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilentz200546-163">&#91;162&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In the presidential election, Jackson won a <a href="/wiki/Plurality_(voting)" title="Plurality (voting)">plurality</a> of the popular vote, receiving 42 percent. More importantly, he won a plurality of <a href="/wiki/Electoral_College_(United_States)" class="mw-redirect" title="Electoral College (United States)">electoral votes</a>, receiving 99 votes from states in the South, West, and Mid-Atlantic. He was the only candidate to win states outside of his regional base: Adams dominated <a href="/wiki/New_England" title="New England">New England</a>, Crawford won Virginia and Georgia, and Clay took three western states. Because no candidate had a <a href="/wiki/Majority_vote" class="mw-redirect" title="Majority vote">majority</a> of 131 of electoral votes, the House of Representatives held a <a href="/wiki/Contingent_election" title="Contingent election">contingent election</a> under the terms of the <a href="/wiki/Twelfth_Amendment_to_the_United_States_Constitution" title="Twelfth Amendment to the United States Constitution">Twelfth Amendment</a>. The amendment specifies that only the top three electoral vote-winners are eligible to be elected by the House, so Clay was eliminated from contention.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERemini198181–83_164-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERemini198181–83-164">&#91;163&#93;</a></sup> Clay, who was also <a href="/wiki/Speaker_of_the_United_States_House_of_Representatives" title="Speaker of the United States House of Representatives">Speaker of the House</a> and presided over the election's resolution, saw a Jackson presidency as a disaster for the country.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWilentz200547_165-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilentz200547-165">&#91;164&#93;</a></sup> Clay threw his support behind Adams, who won the contingent election on the first ballot. Adams appointed Clay as his Secretary of State, leading supporters of Jackson to accuse Clay and Adams of having struck a "<a href="/wiki/Corrupt_bargain" title="Corrupt bargain">corrupt bargain</a>".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWilentz200545–48_166-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilentz200545–48-166">&#91;165&#93;</a></sup> After the Congressional session concluded, Jackson resigned his Senate seat and returned to Tennessee.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWilentz200549_167-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilentz200549-167">&#91;166&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Election_of_1828_and_death_of_Rachel_Jackson">Election of 1828 and death of Rachel Jackson</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main articles: <a href="/wiki/1828_United_States_presidential_election" title="1828 United States presidential election">1828 United States presidential election</a> and <a href="/wiki/Andrew_Jackson_1828_presidential_campaign" title="Andrew Jackson 1828 presidential campaign">Andrew Jackson 1828 presidential campaign</a></div>
<p>After the election, Jackson's supporters formed a new party to undermine Adams and ensure he served only one term. Adams's presidency went poorly, and Adams's behavior undermined it. He was perceived as an intellectual elite who ignored the needs of the populace. He was unable to accomplish anything because Congress blocked his proposals.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEUnger2012245–248_168-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEUnger2012245–248-168">&#91;167&#93;</a></sup> In his First Annual Message to Congress, Adams stated that "we are palsied by the will of our constituents", which was interpreted as his being against representative democracy.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERemini1981110_169-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERemini1981110-169">&#91;168&#93;</a></sup> Jackson responded by championing the needs of ordinary citizens and declaring that "the voice of the people<span class="nowrap">&#160;</span>... must be heard".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEUnger2012246_170-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEUnger2012246-170">&#91;169&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Electoral_Votes_for_1828-_Focus_on_Jackson.png" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="Refer to caption" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f1/Electoral_Votes_for_1828-_Focus_on_Jackson.png/250px-Electoral_Votes_for_1828-_Focus_on_Jackson.png" decoding="async" width="250" height="257" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f1/Electoral_Votes_for_1828-_Focus_on_Jackson.png/375px-Electoral_Votes_for_1828-_Focus_on_Jackson.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f1/Electoral_Votes_for_1828-_Focus_on_Jackson.png/500px-Electoral_Votes_for_1828-_Focus_on_Jackson.png 2x" data-file-width="1993" data-file-height="2048" /></a><figcaption>1828 election results</figcaption></figure>
<p>Jackson was nominated for president by the Tennessee legislature in October 1825, more than three years before the 1828 election.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWilentz200550–51_171-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilentz200550–51-171">&#91;170&#93;</a></sup> He gained powerful supporters in both the south and north, including Calhoun, who became Jackson's vice presidential running mate, and New York Senator <a href="/wiki/Martin_Van_Buren" title="Martin Van Buren">Martin Van Buren</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENiven1988126_172-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENiven1988126-172">&#91;171&#93;</a></sup> Meanwhile, Adams's support from the Southern states was eroded when he signed a tax on European imports, the <a href="/wiki/Tariff_of_Abominations" title="Tariff of Abominations">Tariff of 1828</a>, which was called the "Tariff of Abominations" by opponents, into law.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEUnger2012246_170-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEUnger2012246-170">&#91;169&#93;</a></sup> Jackson's victory in the presidential race was overwhelming. He won 56 percent of the popular vote and 68 percent of the electoral vote. The election ended the <a href="/wiki/One-party_state" title="One-party state">one-party system</a> that had formed during the <a href="/wiki/Era_of_Good_Feelings" title="Era of Good Feelings">Era of Good Feelings</a> as Jackson's supporters coalesced into the <a href="/wiki/Democratic_Party_(United_States)" title="Democratic Party (United States)">Democratic Party</a> and the various groups who did not support him eventually formed the <a href="/wiki/Whig_Party_(United_States)" title="Whig Party (United States)">Whig Party</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKoenig1964197–198_173-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKoenig1964197–198-173">&#91;172&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The political campaign was dominated by the personal abuse that partisans flung at both candidates.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKoenig1964197_174-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKoenig1964197-174">&#91;173&#93;</a></sup> Jackson was accused of being the son of an English prostitute and a <a href="/wiki/Mulatto" title="Mulatto">mulatto</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERemini1977134_175-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERemini1977134-175">&#91;174&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMarszalek199716_176-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMarszalek199716-176">&#91;175&#93;</a></sup> and he was labeled a slave trader who trafficked in human flesh.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECheathem2014§3_177-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECheathem2014§3-177">&#91;176&#93;</a></sup> A series of pamphlets known as the <a href="/wiki/Coffin_Handbills" title="Coffin Handbills">Coffin Handbills</a><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBoller200445–46_178-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBoller200445–46-178">&#91;177&#93;</a></sup> accused him of having murdered 18 white men, including the soldiers he had executed for desertion and alleging that he stabbed a man in the back with his cane.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHowell2010294–295_179-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHowell2010294–295-179">&#91;178&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBinns1828_180-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBinns1828-180">&#91;179&#93;</a></sup> They stated that he had intentionally massacred Native American women and children at the Battle of Horseshoe Bend, ate the bodies of Native Americans he killed in battle,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETaliaferro1828_181-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETaliaferro1828-181">&#91;180&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-182" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-182">&#91;181&#93;</a></sup> and threatened to cut off the ears of congressmen who questioned his behavior during the First Seminole War.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHowell2010295–297_183-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHowell2010295–297-183">&#91;182&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Jackson and Rachel were accused of adultery for living together before her divorce was finalized,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHowe2007277–278_184-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHowe2007277–278-184">&#91;183&#93;</a></sup> and Rachel heard about the accusation.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEUnger2012256_185-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEUnger2012256-185">&#91;184&#93;</a></sup> She had been under stress throughout the election, and just as Jackson was preparing to head to Washington for his inauguration, she fell ill.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBrands2005404–405_186-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrands2005404–405-186">&#91;185&#93;</a></sup> She did not live to see her husband become president, dying of a stroke or heart attack a few days later, and was buried on Christmas Eve.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEUnger2012256_185-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEUnger2012256-185">&#91;184&#93;</a></sup> Jackson believed that the abuse from Adams' supporters had hastened her death, stating at her funeral: "May God Almighty forgive her murderers, as I know she forgave them. I never can."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBoller200446_187-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBoller200446-187">&#91;186&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span id="Presidency_.281829.E2.80.931837.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Presidency_(1829–1837)">Presidency (1829–1837)</span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Presidency_of_Andrew_Jackson" title="Presidency of Andrew Jackson">Presidency of Andrew Jackson</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Andrew_Jackson_Portrait.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="Man stands in white shirt and black pants and coat with right hand on desk and left hand at his side." src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8a/Andrew_Jackson_Portrait.jpg/220px-Andrew_Jackson_Portrait.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="293" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8a/Andrew_Jackson_Portrait.jpg/330px-Andrew_Jackson_Portrait.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8a/Andrew_Jackson_Portrait.jpg/440px-Andrew_Jackson_Portrait.jpg 2x" data-file-width="5770" data-file-height="7690" /></a><figcaption>Engraving of President Jackson by <a href="/wiki/Alexander_Hay_Ritchie" title="Alexander Hay Ritchie">A. H. Ritchie</a> after painting by <a href="/wiki/Dennis_Malone_Carter" title="Dennis Malone Carter">D. M. Carter</a> (<abbr title="circa">c.</abbr> 1860)</figcaption></figure>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Inauguration">Inauguration</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/First_inauguration_of_Andrew_Jackson" title="First inauguration of Andrew Jackson">First inauguration of Andrew Jackson</a></div>
<p>Jackson arrived in Washington on February 11. His first concern was forming his cabinet.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERemini1981150_188-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERemini1981150-188">&#91;187&#93;</a></sup> He chose Van Buren as Secretary of State, his friend John Eaton as Secretary of War, <a href="/wiki/Samuel_D._Ingham" title="Samuel D. Ingham">Samuel D. Ingham</a> as Secretary of Treasury, <a href="/wiki/John_Branch" title="John Branch">John Branch</a> as Secretary of Navy, <a href="/wiki/John_M._Berrien" title="John M. Berrien">John M. Berrien</a> as Attorney General, and <a href="/wiki/William_T._Barry" title="William T. Barry">William T. Barry</a> as Postmaster General.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELatner2002105_189-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELatner2002105-189">&#91;188&#93;</a></sup> Jackson was inaugurated on March 4, 1829, becoming the first president-elect to take the oath of office on the East Portico of the U.S. Capitol.<sup id="cite_ref-190" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-190">&#91;189&#93;</a></sup> Embittered by his defeat, Adams refused to attend.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEUnger2012256–257_191-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEUnger2012256–257-191">&#91;190&#93;</a></sup> In his inaugural address, Jackson promised to protect the sovereignty of the states, respect the limits of the presidency, reform the government by removing disloyal or incompetent appointees, and observe a fair policy toward Native Americans.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEJackson1829_192-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEJackson1829-192">&#91;191&#93;</a></sup> Jackson invited the public to the <a href="/wiki/White_House" title="White House">White House</a>, which was promptly overrun by well-wishers who caused minor damage to its furnishings. The spectacle earned him the nickname "King Mob".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWilentz200555_193-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilentz200555-193">&#91;192&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Reforms_and_rotation_in_office">Reforms and rotation in office</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Further information: <a href="/wiki/Spoils_system" title="Spoils system">Spoils system</a></div>
<p>Jackson's administration believed that Adams's had been corrupt and one of Jackson's first acts as president was to initiate investigations into all executive departments.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGilman199564–65_194-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGilman199564–65-194">&#91;193&#93;</a></sup> The investigations revealed that $280,000 (equivalent to $7,700,000&#32;in 2022) was stolen from the Treasury, and the reduction in costs to the Department of the Navy saved it $1&#160;million (equivalent to $27,500,000&#32;in 2022).<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERemini1981186–187_195-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERemini1981186–187-195">&#91;194&#93;</a></sup> One of the people caught in his investigation was the <a href="/wiki/United_States_Department_of_the_Treasury" title="United States Department of the Treasury">Treasury</a> Auditor <a href="/wiki/Tobias_Watkins" title="Tobias Watkins">Tobias Watkins</a>, a personal friend of Adams' who was found guilty of embezzlement.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGilman199564_196-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGilman199564-196">&#91;195&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERemini1981186–187_195-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERemini1981186–187-195">&#91;194&#93;</a></sup> In the first year of his term Jackson asked Congress to tighten laws on embezzlement, revise laws to reduce tax evasion, and pushed for an improved government accounting system.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEllis197456_197-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEllis197456-197">&#91;196&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Jackson implemented a principle he called "<a href="/wiki/Term_limits_in_the_United_States" title="Term limits in the United States">rotation in office</a>" by enforcing the <a href="/wiki/Tenure_of_Office_Act_(1820)" title="Tenure of Office Act (1820)">Tenure of Office Act</a>, signed by President <a href="/wiki/James_Monroe" title="James Monroe">Monroe</a> in 1820, that limited appointed office tenure and authorized the president to remove and appoint political party associates. The previous custom had been for the president to leave the existing appointees in office, replacing them through attrition.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHowe2007332–333_198-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHowe2007332–333-198">&#91;197&#93;</a></sup> During his first year in office, Jackson removed hundreds of federal officials, which was about 10% of all Federal employees,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHowe2007332–333_198-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHowe2007332–333-198">&#91;197&#93;</a></sup> and replaced them with loyal Democrats.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESabatoO&#39;Connor2002293_199-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESabatoO&#39;Connor2002293-199">&#91;198&#93;</a></sup> He argued that rotation in office was a democratic reform that reduced bureaucracy and corruption<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFriedrich193714_200-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFriedrich193714-200">&#91;199&#93;</a></sup> by preventing hereditary officeholding and made officeholders responsible to the popular will,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEllis197451_201-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEllis197451-201">&#91;200&#93;</a></sup> but it functioned as political patronage, which came to be known as the <a href="/wiki/Spoils_system" title="Spoils system">spoils system</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEllis197461_202-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEllis197461-202">&#91;201&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFriedrich193714_200-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFriedrich193714-200">&#91;199&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Petticoat_affair">Petticoat affair</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Petticoat_affair" title="Petticoat affair">Petticoat affair</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Pettycoat_Affair_during_Andrew_Jackson%27s_first_administration.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="Jackson faces a woman dancing, flanked by three seated men on right; three seated men on left and one man standing behind the woman" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/35/Pettycoat_Affair_during_Andrew_Jackson%27s_first_administration.jpg/220px-Pettycoat_Affair_during_Andrew_Jackson%27s_first_administration.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="151" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/35/Pettycoat_Affair_during_Andrew_Jackson%27s_first_administration.jpg/330px-Pettycoat_Affair_during_Andrew_Jackson%27s_first_administration.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/35/Pettycoat_Affair_during_Andrew_Jackson%27s_first_administration.jpg/440px-Pettycoat_Affair_during_Andrew_Jackson%27s_first_administration.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1939" data-file-height="1333" /></a><figcaption>Lithograph cartoon <i>The Celeste-al Cabinet</i> by Albert A. Hoffay, published by <a href="/wiki/Henry_R._Robinson_(publisher)" title="Henry R. Robinson (publisher)">H. R. Robinson</a> (1836). Illustrates Jackson's cabinet during the Petticoat Affair. "Celeste" is <a href="/wiki/Peggy_Eaton" title="Peggy Eaton">Margaret Eaton</a>.</figcaption></figure>
<p>Jackson spent much of his time during his first two and a half years in office dealing with what came to be known as the "Petticoat Affair" or "Eaton Affair".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWood1997238_203-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWood1997238-203">&#91;202&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMarszalek1997vii_204-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMarszalek1997vii-204">&#91;203&#93;</a></sup> The affair focused on Secretary of War Eaton's wife, <a href="/wiki/Peggy_Eaton" title="Peggy Eaton">Margaret</a>. She had a reputation for being promiscuous, and like Rachel Jackson, she was accused of adultery. She and Eaton had been close before her first husband <a href="/wiki/John_B._Timberlake" title="John B. Timberlake">John Timberlake</a> died, and they married nine months after his death.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMeacham200866–67_205-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMeacham200866–67-205">&#91;204&#93;</a></sup> With the exception of Barry's wife Catherine,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHowe2007336_206-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHowe2007336-206">&#91;205&#93;</a></sup> the cabinet members' wives followed the lead of Vice-president Calhoun's wife <a href="/wiki/Floride_Calhoun" title="Floride Calhoun">Floride</a> and refused to socialize with the Eatons.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMarszalek199753–55_207-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMarszalek199753–55-207">&#91;206&#93;</a></sup> Though Jackson defended Margaret, her presence split the cabinet, which had been so ineffective that he rarely called it into session,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELatner2002105_189-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELatner2002105-189">&#91;188&#93;</a></sup> and the ongoing disagreement led to its dissolution.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWood1997239–241_208-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWood1997239–241-208">&#91;207&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In the spring of 1831, Jackson demanded the resignations of all the cabinet members except Barry,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELatner2002108_209-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELatner2002108-209">&#91;208&#93;</a></sup> who would resign in 1835 when a Congressional investigation revealed his mismanagement of the Post Office.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERemini1984240–243_210-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERemini1984240–243-210">&#91;209&#93;</a></sup> Jackson tried to compensate Van Buren by appointing him the <a href="/wiki/List_of_ambassadors_of_the_United_States_to_the_United_Kingdom#Duties" title="List of ambassadors of the United States to the United Kingdom">Minister to Great Britain</a>, but Calhoun blocked the nomination with a tie-breaking vote against it.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELatner2002108_209-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELatner2002108-209">&#91;208&#93;</a></sup> Van Buren—along with <a href="/wiki/Amos_Kendall" title="Amos Kendall">Amos Kendall</a>, who helped organize what would become the Democratic Party,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECole199724_211-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECole199724-211">&#91;210&#93;</a></sup> and <a href="/wiki/Francis_Preston_Blair" title="Francis Preston Blair">Francis Preston Blair</a>, the editor of <i>The Globe</i> newspaper that served as Jackson's <a href="/wiki/House_organ" title="House organ">house organ</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMeacham2008165_212-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMeacham2008165-212">&#91;211&#93;</a></sup>—would become regular participants in Jackson's <a href="/wiki/Kitchen_Cabinet" title="Kitchen Cabinet">Kitchen Cabinet</a>, an unofficial, varying group of advisors that Jackson turned to for decision making even after he had formed a new official cabinet.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELatner1978380–385_213-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELatner1978380–385-213">&#91;212&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Indian_Removal_Act">Indian Removal Act</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Further information: <a href="/wiki/Indian_removal" title="Indian removal">Indian removal</a>, <a href="/wiki/Indian_Removal_Act" title="Indian Removal Act">Indian Removal Act</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Trail_of_Tears" title="Trail of Tears">Trail of Tears</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Cessions_Southeastern_Tribes-_Andrew_Jackson.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="Map of the southern United States showing in dark green areas ceded by Indians.[213]" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/70/Cessions_Southeastern_Tribes-_Andrew_Jackson.jpg/260px-Cessions_Southeastern_Tribes-_Andrew_Jackson.jpg" decoding="async" width="260" height="194" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/70/Cessions_Southeastern_Tribes-_Andrew_Jackson.jpg/390px-Cessions_Southeastern_Tribes-_Andrew_Jackson.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/70/Cessions_Southeastern_Tribes-_Andrew_Jackson.jpg/520px-Cessions_Southeastern_Tribes-_Andrew_Jackson.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2030" data-file-height="1512" /></a><figcaption>The Indian Removal Act and treaties involving Jackson before his presidency displaced most of the <a href="/wiki/Five_Civilized_Tribes" title="Five Civilized Tribes">major tribes of the Southeast</a> from their traditional territories east of the Mississippi.</figcaption></figure>
<p>Jackson's presidency marked the beginning of a national policy of Native American removal.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELatner2002108_209-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELatner2002108-209">&#91;208&#93;</a></sup> Before Jackson took office, the relationship between the southern states and the Native American tribes who lived within their boundaries was strained. The states felt that they had full jurisdiction over their territories; the native tribes saw themselves as autonomous nations that had a right to the land they lived on.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEParsons1973353–358_215-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEParsons1973353–358-215">&#91;214&#93;</a></sup> Significant portions of the <a href="/wiki/Five_Civilized_Tribes" title="Five Civilized Tribes">five major tribes</a> in the area then known as the <a href="/wiki/Old_Southwest" title="Old Southwest">Southwest</a>—the Cherokee, Choctaw, Chickasaw, Creek, and Seminoles— began to adopt white culture, including education, agricultural techniques, a road system, and rudimentary manufacturing.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWallace199358–62_216-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWallace199358–62-216">&#91;215&#93;</a></sup> In the case of the tensions between the state of Georgia and the Cherokee, Adams had tried to address the issue encouraging Cherokee emigration west of the Mississippi through financial incentives, but most refused.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMcLoughlin1986611–612_217-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMcLoughlin1986611–612-217">&#91;216&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In the first days of Jackson's presidency, some of the southern states had passed legislation extending state jurisdiction to Native American lands.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESatz197412_218-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESatz197412-218">&#91;217&#93;</a></sup> Jackson supported the states' right to do so.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECave20031332_219-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECave20031332-219">&#91;218&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERogin1975212–213_220-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERogin1975212–213-220">&#91;219&#93;</a></sup> His position was later made clear in the 1832 Supreme Court <a href="/wiki/Test_case_(law)" title="Test case (law)">test case</a> of this legislation, <i><a href="/wiki/Worcester_v._Georgia" title="Worcester v. Georgia">Worcester v. Georgia</a></i>. Georgia had arrested a group of missionaries for entering Cherokee territory without a permit; the Cherokee declared these arrests illegal. The court under <a href="/wiki/Chief_Justice_of_the_United_States" title="Chief Justice of the United States">Chief Justice</a> <a href="/wiki/John_Marshall" title="John Marshall">John Marshall</a> decided in favor of the Cherokee: imposition of Georgia law on the Cherokee was unconstitutional.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERemini1981276_221-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERemini1981276-221">&#91;220&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Horace_Greeley" title="Horace Greeley">Horace Greeley</a> alleges that when Jackson heard the ruling, he said, "Well, John Marshall has made his decision, but now let him enforce it."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGreeley1864106_222-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGreeley1864106-222">&#91;221&#93;</a></sup> Although the quote may be apocryphal, Jackson made it clear he would not use the federal government to enforce the ruling.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBerutti1992305–306_223-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBerutti1992305–306-223">&#91;222&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMiles1992527–528_224-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMiles1992527–528-224">&#91;223&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWilentz2005141_225-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilentz2005141-225">&#91;224&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Jackson used the power of the federal government to enforce the separation of the Native American tribes and whites.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEParsons1973360_226-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEParsons1973360-226">&#91;225&#93;</a></sup> In May 1830, Jackson passed the <a href="/wiki/Indian_Removal_Act" title="Indian Removal Act">Indian Removal Act</a> through Congress.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELatner2002109_227-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELatner2002109-227">&#91;226&#93;</a></sup> It gave the president the right to negotiate treaties to buy tribal lands in the eastern part of the United States in exchange for lands set aside for Native Americans west of the Mississippi,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWallace199366_228-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWallace199366-228">&#91;227&#93;</a></sup> as well as broad discretion on how to use the federal funds allocated to the negotiations.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDavis201054–55_229-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDavis201054–55-229">&#91;228&#93;</a></sup> The law was supposed to be a voluntary relocation program, but it was not implemented as one. Jackson's administration often achieved agreement to relocate through bribes, fraud and intimidation,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECave20031337_230-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECave20031337-230">&#91;229&#93;</a></sup> and the leaders who signed the treaties often did not represent the entire tribe.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELatner2002110_231-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELatner2002110-231">&#91;230&#93;</a></sup> The relocations could be a source of misery too: the Choctaw relocation was rife with corruption, theft, and mismanagement that brought great suffering to that people.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERemini1981203_232-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERemini1981203-232">&#91;231&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Ralph_Eleaser_Whiteside_Earl_Andrew_Jackson_NCMOA.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/38/Ralph_Eleaser_Whiteside_Earl_Andrew_Jackson_NCMOA.jpg/220px-Ralph_Eleaser_Whiteside_Earl_Andrew_Jackson_NCMOA.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="267" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/38/Ralph_Eleaser_Whiteside_Earl_Andrew_Jackson_NCMOA.jpg/330px-Ralph_Eleaser_Whiteside_Earl_Andrew_Jackson_NCMOA.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/38/Ralph_Eleaser_Whiteside_Earl_Andrew_Jackson_NCMOA.jpg/440px-Ralph_Eleaser_Whiteside_Earl_Andrew_Jackson_NCMOA.jpg 2x" data-file-width="527" data-file-height="640" /></a><figcaption>Portrait of Jackson by <a href="/wiki/Ralph_Eleaser_Whiteside_Earl" title="Ralph Eleaser Whiteside Earl">Ralph E. W. Earl</a> (<abbr title="circa">c.</abbr> 1830, <a href="/wiki/North_Carolina_Museum_of_Art" title="North Carolina Museum of Art">North Carolina Museum of Art</a>)</figcaption></figure>
<p>In 1830, Jackson personally negotiated with the Chickasaw, who quickly agreed to move.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERemini1981271_233-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERemini1981271-233">&#91;232&#93;</a></sup> In the same year, Choctaw leaders signed the <a href="/wiki/Treaty_of_Dancing_Rabbit_Creek" title="Treaty of Dancing Rabbit Creek">Treaty of Dancing Rabbit Creek</a>; the majority did not want the treaty but complied with its terms.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHowe2007353_234-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHowe2007353-234">&#91;233&#93;</a></sup> In 1832, Seminole leaders signed the <a href="/wiki/Treaty_of_Payne%27s_Landing" title="Treaty of Payne&#39;s Landing">Treaty of Payne's Landing</a>, which stipulated that the Seminoles would move west and become part of the Muscogee Creek Confederacy if they found the new land suitable.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMissallMissall200483–85_235-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMissallMissall200483–85-235">&#91;234&#93;</a></sup> Most Seminoles refused to move, leading to the <a href="/wiki/Second_Seminole_War" title="Second Seminole War">Second Seminole War</a> in 1835 that lasted six years.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELatner2002110_231-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELatner2002110-231">&#91;230&#93;</a></sup> Members of the Muscogee Creek Confederacy ceded their land to the state of Alabama in the <a href="/wiki/Treaty_of_Cusseta" title="Treaty of Cusseta">Treaty of Cusseta</a> of 1832. Their private ownership of the land was to be protected, but the federal government did not enforce this. The government did encourage voluntary removal until the <a href="/wiki/Creek_War_of_1836" title="Creek War of 1836">Creek War of 1836</a>, after which almost all Creek were removed to Oklahoma territory.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHaveman20091–5,_129_236-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHaveman20091–5,_129-236">&#91;235&#93;</a></sup> In 1836, Cherokee leaders ceded their land to the government by the <a href="/wiki/Treaty_of_New_Echota" title="Treaty of New Echota">Treaty of New Echota</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHowe2007415_237-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHowe2007415-237">&#91;236&#93;</a></sup> Their removal, known as the <a href="/wiki/Trail_of_Tears" title="Trail of Tears">Trail of Tears</a>, was enforced by Jackson's successor, Van Buren.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBrands2005536_238-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrands2005536-238">&#91;237&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Jackson also applied the removal policy in the <a href="/wiki/Northwest_Territory" title="Northwest Territory">Northwest</a>. He was not successful in removing the <a href="/wiki/Iroquois" title="Iroquois">Iroquois Confederacy</a> in New York, but when some members of the <a href="/wiki/Meskwaki" title="Meskwaki">Meskwaki</a> (Fox) and the <a href="/wiki/Sauk_people" title="Sauk people">Sauk</a> triggered the <a href="/wiki/Black_Hawk_War" title="Black Hawk War">Black Hawk War</a> by trying to cross back to the east side of the Mississippi, the peace treaties ratified after their defeat reduced their lands further.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHowe2007418–419_239-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHowe2007418–419-239">&#91;238&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>During his administration, he made about 70 treaties with American Indian tribes. He had removed almost all the Native Americans east of the Mississippi and south of Lake Michigan, about 70,000 people, from the United States;<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERogin1975206_240-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERogin1975206-240">&#91;239&#93;</a></sup> though it was done at the cost of thousands of Native American lives lost because of the unsanitary conditions and epidemics arising from their dislocation, as well as their resistance to expulsion.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEOstler2019&#91;httpsbooksgooglecombooksid6zeWDwAAQBAJpgPA256_256&#93;,_&#91;httpsbooksgooglecombooksid6zeWDwAAQBAJpgPA263_263&#93;,_&#91;httpsbooksgooglecombooksid6zeWDwAAQBAJpgPA273_273&#93;–274,_&#91;httpsbooksgooglecombooksid6zeWDwAAQBAJpgPA280_280&#93;_241-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEOstler2019[httpsbooksgooglecombooksid6zeWDwAAQBAJpgPA256_256],_[httpsbooksgooglecombooksid6zeWDwAAQBAJpgPA263_263],_[httpsbooksgooglecombooksid6zeWDwAAQBAJpgPA273_273]–274,_[httpsbooksgooglecombooksid6zeWDwAAQBAJpgPA280_280]-241">&#91;240&#93;</a></sup> Jackson's implementation of the Indian Removal Act contributed to his popularity with his constituency. He added over 170,000 square miles of land to the public domain, which primarily benefited the United States' agricultural interests. The act also benefited small farmers, as Jackson allowed them to purchase moderate plots at low prices and offered squatters on land formerly belonging to Native Americans the option to purchase it before it was offered for sale to others.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWhapples2014546–548_242-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWhapples2014546–548-242">&#91;241&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Nullification_crisis">Nullification crisis</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Nullification_crisis" title="Nullification crisis">Nullification crisis</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Democracy-1832-1864-Jackson.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="Jackson stands looking right with right arm raised; Calhoun faces left bowing before Jackson with hands down." src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/18/Democracy-1832-1864-Jackson.jpg/220px-Democracy-1832-1864-Jackson.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="306" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/18/Democracy-1832-1864-Jackson.jpg/330px-Democracy-1832-1864-Jackson.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/18/Democracy-1832-1864-Jackson.jpg/440px-Democracy-1832-1864-Jackson.jpg 2x" data-file-width="704" data-file-height="980" /></a><figcaption>Civil War-era lithograph cartoon of Calhoun bowing before Jackson during the nullification crisis by <a href="/wiki/Pendleton%27s_Lithography" title="Pendleton&#39;s Lithography">Pendleton's Lithography</a> and published by <a href="/wiki/Louis_Prang" title="Louis Prang">L. Prang &amp; Co.</a> (1864)</figcaption></figure>
<p>Jackson had to confront another challenge that had been building up since the beginning of his first term. The Tariff of 1828, which had been passed in the last year of Adams' administration, set a protective tariff at a very high rate to prevent the manufacturing industries in the Northern states from having to compete with lower-priced imports from Britain.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWilentz200563–64_243-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilentz200563–64-243">&#91;242&#93;</a></sup> The tariff reduced the income of southern cotton planters: it propped up consumer prices, but not the price of cotton which had severely declined in the previous decade.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFreehling19666_244-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFreehling19666-244">&#91;243&#93;</a></sup> Immediately after the tariff's passage, the <i><a href="/wiki/South_Carolina_Exposition_and_Protest" title="South Carolina Exposition and Protest">South Carolina Exposition and Protest</a></i> was sent to the U.S. Senate.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBrogdon2011245–273_245-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrogdon2011245–273-245">&#91;244&#93;</a></sup> This document, which had been anonymously written by John C. Calhoun, asserted that the constitution was a compact of individual states<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWilentz200564_246-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilentz200564-246">&#91;245&#93;</a></sup> and when the federal government went beyond its delegated duties, such as enacting a protective tariff, a state had a right to declare this action unconstitutional and make the act null and void with the borders of that state.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEllis19897–8_247-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEllis19897–8-247">&#91;246&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Jackson suspected Calhoun of writing the <i>Exposition and Protest</i>, and opposed his interpretation. Jackson argued that Congress as a whole had full authority to enact tariffs and that a dissenting state was denying the will of the majority.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWilentz200564–65_248-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilentz200564–65-248">&#91;247&#93;</a></sup> He also needed the tariff, which generated 90% of the federal revenue,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETemin196929_249-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETemin196929-249">&#91;248&#93;</a></sup> to achieve another of his presidential goals, eliminating the national debt.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELane2014121–122_250-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELane2014121–122-250">&#91;249&#93;</a></sup> The issue developed into a personal rivalry between the two men. For example, during a celebration of Thomas Jefferson's birthday on April 13, 1830, the attendees gave after-dinner toasts. Jackson toasted: "Our federal Union: It must be preserved!"&#160;– a clear challenge to nullification. Calhoun whose toast immediately followed, rebutted: "The Union: Next to our Liberty, the most dear!"<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBrands2005445–446_251-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrands2005445–446-251">&#91;250&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>As a compromise, Jackson supported the <a href="/wiki/Tariff_of_1832" title="Tariff of 1832">Tariff of 1832</a>, which reduced the duties from the Tariff of 1828 by almost half. The bill was signed on July 9, but failed to satisfy extremists on either side.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERemini1981358–360_252-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERemini1981358–360-252">&#91;251&#93;</a></sup> On November 24, South Carolina had passed the <a href="/wiki/Ordinance_of_Nullification" title="Ordinance of Nullification">Ordinance of Nullification</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBergeron1976263_253-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBergeron1976263-253">&#91;252&#93;</a></sup> declaring both tariffs null and void and threatening to secede from the United States if the federal government tried to use force to collect the duties.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFreehling19661–2_254-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFreehling19661–2-254">&#91;253&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEOrdinance_of_Nullification1832_255-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEOrdinance_of_Nullification1832-255">&#91;254&#93;</a></sup> In response, Jackson sent warships to Charleston harbor, and threatened to hang any man who worked to support nullification or secession.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHowe2007404–406_256-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHowe2007404–406-256">&#91;255&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On December 10, Jackson issued a proclamation against the "nullifiers",<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERemini198422_257-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERemini198422-257">&#91;256&#93;</a></sup> stating that he considered "the power to annul a law of the United States, assumed by one State, incompatible with the existence of the Union, contradicted expressly by the letter of the Constitution, unauthorized by its spirit, inconsistent with every principle on which it was founded, and destructive of the great object for which it was formed". South Carolina, the president declared, stood on "the brink of insurrection and treason", and he appealed to the people of the state to reassert their allegiance to that Union. Jackson also denied the right of secession: "The Constitution&#160;... forms a government not a league&#160;... To say that any State may at pleasure secede from the Union is to say that the United States are not a nation."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEJackson1832_258-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEJackson1832-258">&#91;257&#93;</a></sup> On December 28, Calhoun, who had been elected to the U.S. Senate, resigned as vice president.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFeerick196585–86_259-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFeerick196585–86-259">&#91;258&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Jackson asked Congress to pass a "<a href="/wiki/Force_Bill" title="Force Bill">Force Bill</a>" authorizing the military to enforce the tariff. It was attacked by Calhoun as despotism.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMeacham2008239–240_260-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMeacham2008239–240-260">&#91;259&#93;</a></sup> Meanwhile, Calhoun and Clay began to work on a new <a href="/wiki/Tariff_of_1833" title="Tariff of 1833">compromise tariff</a>. Jackson saw it as an effective way to end the confrontation, but insisted on the passage of the Force Bill before he signed.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEricson1995253,_fn14_261-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEricson1995253,_fn14-261">&#91;260&#93;</a></sup> On March 2, he signed into law the Force Bill and the Tariff of 1833, both of which passed on March 1, 1833. The South Carolina Convention then met and rescinded its nullification ordinance, but nullified the Force Bill in a final act of defiance.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERemini198442_262-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERemini198442-262">&#91;261&#93;</a></sup> Two months later, Jackson reflected on South Carolina's nullification: "the tariff was only the pretext, and <a href="/wiki/Secession_in_the_United_States" title="Secession in the United States">disunion</a> and <a href="/wiki/Confederate_States_of_America" title="Confederate States of America">southern confederacy</a> the real object. The next pretext will be the negro, or <a href="/wiki/History_of_slavery_in_the_United_States" class="mw-redirect" title="History of slavery in the United States">slavery</a> question".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMeacham2008247_263-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMeacham2008247-263">&#91;262&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Bank_War_and_Election_of_1832">Bank War and Election of 1832</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main articles: <a href="/wiki/Bank_War" title="Bank War">Bank War</a> and <a href="/wiki/Banking_in_the_Jacksonian_Era" title="Banking in the Jacksonian Era">Banking in the Jacksonian Era</a></div>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Bank_Veto">Bank Veto</span></h4>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Downfall_of_Mother_Bank.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="Jackson holds up document towards a devil and other people who flee while columns tumble around them." src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/fa/Downfall_of_Mother_Bank.jpg/220px-Downfall_of_Mother_Bank.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="153" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/fa/Downfall_of_Mother_Bank.jpg/330px-Downfall_of_Mother_Bank.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/fa/Downfall_of_Mother_Bank.jpg/440px-Downfall_of_Mother_Bank.jpg 2x" data-file-width="8096" data-file-height="5648" /></a><figcaption>Lithograph cartoon of Jackson destroying the <a href="/wiki/Second_Bank_of_the_United_States" title="Second Bank of the United States">Second Bank of the United States</a> with his "<a href="/wiki/Bank_War#Removal_of_the_deposits_and_panic_of_1833–34" title="Bank War">Removal Notice</a>" by Zachariah Downing, published by <a href="/wiki/Henry_R._Robinson_(publisher)" title="Henry R. Robinson (publisher)">H. R. Robinson</a> (<abbr title="circa">c.</abbr> 1833). <a href="/wiki/Nicholas_Biddle" title="Nicholas Biddle">Nicholas Biddle</a> is portrayed as the devil.</figcaption></figure>
<p>A few weeks after his inauguration, Jackson started looking into how he could replace the Second Bank of the United States.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWilentz200574_264-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilentz200574-264">&#91;263&#93;</a></sup> The Bank had been chartered by President Madison in 1816 to restore the United States economy after the War of 1812. Monroe had appointed <a href="/wiki/Nicholas_Biddle_(banker)" class="mw-redirect" title="Nicholas Biddle (banker)">Nicholas Biddle</a> as the Bank's executive.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELatner2002111_265-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELatner2002111-265">&#91;264&#93;</a></sup> The Bank was a repository for the country's public monies which also serviced the national debt; it was formed as a for-profit entity that looked after the concerns of its shareholders.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECampbell2016273,_277_266-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECampbell2016273,_277-266">&#91;265&#93;</a></sup> Under the Bank's stewardship, the country was economically healthy<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHowe2007375–376_267-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHowe2007375–376-267">&#91;266&#93;</a></sup> and the currency was stable,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHammond1957374_268-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHammond1957374-268">&#91;267&#93;</a></sup> but Jackson saw the Bank as a fourth branch of government run by an elite,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWilentz200574_264-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilentz200574-264">&#91;263&#93;</a></sup> what he called the "money power" that sought to control the labor and earnings of the "real people", who depend on their own efforts to succeed: the planters, farmers, mechanics, and laborers.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMeyers196020–24_269-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMeyers196020–24-269">&#91;268&#93;</a></sup> Additionally, Jackson's own near bankruptcy in 1804 due to credit-fuelled land speculation had biased him against paper money and toward a policy favorable to <a href="/wiki/Hard_money_(policy)" title="Hard money (policy)">hard money</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESellers195461–84_270-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESellers195461–84-270">&#91;269&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In his <a href="/wiki/1829_State_of_the_Union_Address" title="1829 State of the Union Address">First Annual Address</a> in December 1829, Jackson openly challenged the Bank by questioning its constitutionality and the soundness of its money.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPerkins1987532–533_271-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPerkins1987532–533-271">&#91;270&#93;</a></sup> Jackson's supporters further alleged that it gave preferential loans to speculators and merchants over artisans and farmers, that it used its money to bribe congressmen and the press, and that it had ties with foreign creditors. Biddle responded to Jackson's challenge in early 1830 by using the Bank's vast financial holding to ensure the Bank's reputation, and his supporters argued that the Bank was the key to prosperity and stable commerce. By the time of the 1832 election, Biddle had spent over $250,000, (equivalent to $7,328,300&#32;in 2022), in printing pamphlets, lobbying for pro-Bank legislation, hiring agents and giving loans to editors and congressmen.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECampbell2016274–278_272-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECampbell2016274–278-272">&#91;271&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On the surface, Jackson's and Biddle's positions did not appear irreconcilable. Jackson seemed open to keeping the Bank if it could include some degree of Federal oversight, limit its real estate holdings, and have its property subject to taxation by the states.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPerkins1987534–535_273-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPerkins1987534–535-273">&#91;272&#93;</a></sup> Many of Jackson's cabinet members thought a compromise was possible. In 1831, Treasury Secretary <a href="/wiki/Louis_McLane" title="Louis McLane">Louis McLane</a> told Biddle that Jackson was open to chartering a modified version of the Bank, but Biddle did not consult Jackson directly. Privately, Jackson expressed opposition to the Bank;<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECampbell2016285_274-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECampbell2016285-274">&#91;273&#93;</a></sup> publicly, he announced that he would leave the decision concerning the Bank in the hands of the people.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWilentz2005285_275-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilentz2005285-275">&#91;274&#93;</a></sup> Biddle was finally convinced to take open action by Henry Clay, who had decided to run for president against Jackson in the 1832 election. Biddle would agree to seek renewal of the charter two years earlier than scheduled. Clay argued that Jackson was in a bind. If he vetoed the charter, he would lose the votes of his pro-Bank constituents in Pennsylvania; but if he signed the charter, he would lose his anti-Bank constituents. After the recharter bill was passed, Jackson vetoed it on July 10, 1832, arguing that the country should not surrender the will of the majority to the desires of the wealthy.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBaptist2016260_276-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBaptist2016260-276">&#91;275&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Election_of_1832">Election of 1832</span></h4>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/1832_United_States_presidential_election" title="1832 United States presidential election">1832 United States presidential election</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Electoral_Votes_for_1832-_Focus_on_Jackson.png" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="A map of the 1832 presidential election. Blue states were won by Jackson." src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/14/Electoral_Votes_for_1832-_Focus_on_Jackson.png/220px-Electoral_Votes_for_1832-_Focus_on_Jackson.png" decoding="async" width="220" height="226" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/14/Electoral_Votes_for_1832-_Focus_on_Jackson.png/330px-Electoral_Votes_for_1832-_Focus_on_Jackson.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/14/Electoral_Votes_for_1832-_Focus_on_Jackson.png/440px-Electoral_Votes_for_1832-_Focus_on_Jackson.png 2x" data-file-width="1993" data-file-height="2048" /></a><figcaption>1832 election results</figcaption></figure>
<p>The <a href="/wiki/1832_United_States_presidential_election" title="1832 United States presidential election">1832 presidential election</a> demonstrated the rapid development of political parties during Jackson's presidency. The Democratic Party's first national convention, held in Baltimore, nominated Jackson's choice for vice president, Martin Van Buren. The National Republican Party, which had held its first convention in Baltimore earlier in December 1831, nominated Clay, now a senator from Kentucky, and <a href="/wiki/John_Sergeant_(politician)" title="John Sergeant (politician)">John Sergeant</a> of Pennsylvania.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMeacham2008218_277-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMeacham2008218-277">&#91;276&#93;</a></sup> An <a href="/wiki/Anti-Masonic_Party" title="Anti-Masonic Party">Anti-Masonic Party</a> with a platform built around opposition to Freemasonry,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMeacham2008420_278-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMeacham2008420-278">&#91;277&#93;</a></sup> it supported neither Jackson nor Clay, who both were Masons. The party nominated <a href="/wiki/William_Wirt_(Attorney_General)" class="mw-redirect" title="William Wirt (Attorney General)">William Wirt</a> of Maryland and <a href="/wiki/Amos_Ellmaker" title="Amos Ellmaker">Amos Ellmaker</a> of Pennsylvania.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELatner2002112–113_279-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELatner2002112–113-279">&#91;278&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In addition to the votes Jackson would lose because of the Bank veto, Clay hoped that Jackson's Indian Removal Act would alienate voters in the East; but Jackson's losses were offset by the Act's popularity in the West and Southwest. Clay had also expected that Jackson would lose votes because of his stand on internal improvements.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGammon192255–56_280-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGammon192255–56-280">&#91;279&#93;</a></sup> Jackson had <a href="/wiki/Maysville_Road_veto" title="Maysville Road veto">vetoed the Maysville Road bill</a>, which funded an upgrade of a section of the <a href="/wiki/National_Road" title="National Road">National Road</a> in Clay's state of Kentucky; as part of his justification, Jackson claimed it was unconstitutional to fund internal improvements using national funds for local projects.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEJackson1966261–268_281-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEJackson1966261–268-281">&#91;280&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Clay's strategy failed. Jackson was able to mobilize the Democratic Party's strong political networks.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELatner2002113_282-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELatner2002113-282">&#91;281&#93;</a></sup> The Northeast supported Jackson because he was in favor of maintaining a stiff tariff; the West supported him because the Indian Removal Act reduced the number of Native Americans in the region and made available more public land.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEVan_Deusen196354_283-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEVan_Deusen196354-283">&#91;282&#93;</a></sup> Except for South Carolina, which passed the Ordinance of Nullification during the election month and refused to support any party by giving its votes to the future Governor of Virginia <a href="/wiki/John_B._Floyd" title="John B. Floyd">John B. Floyd</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEricson1995259_284-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEricson1995259-284">&#91;283&#93;</a></sup> the South supported Jackson for implementing the Indian Removal Act, as well as for his willingness to compromise by signing the Tariff of 1832.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERatcliffe200010–14_285-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERatcliffe200010–14-285">&#91;284&#93;</a></sup> Jackson won the election by a landslide, receiving 55 percent of the popular vote and 219 electoral votes.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELatner2002113_282-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELatner2002113-282">&#91;281&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Removal_of_deposits_and_censure">Removal of deposits and censure</span></h4>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">See also: <a href="/wiki/Censure_of_Andrew_Jackson" title="Censure of Andrew Jackson">Censure of Andrew Jackson</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:King_Andrew_the_First_(political_cartoon_of_President_Andrew_Jackson).jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="Jackson dressed as king with robe and crown, veto in hand and stepping on the Constitution" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/32/King_Andrew_the_First_%28political_cartoon_of_President_Andrew_Jackson%29.jpg/220px-King_Andrew_the_First_%28political_cartoon_of_President_Andrew_Jackson%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="312" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/32/King_Andrew_the_First_%28political_cartoon_of_President_Andrew_Jackson%29.jpg/330px-King_Andrew_the_First_%28political_cartoon_of_President_Andrew_Jackson%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/32/King_Andrew_the_First_%28political_cartoon_of_President_Andrew_Jackson%29.jpg/440px-King_Andrew_the_First_%28political_cartoon_of_President_Andrew_Jackson%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1471" data-file-height="2089" /></a><figcaption>Lithograph cartoon of "King Andrew the First" by an anonymous artist (<abbr title="circa">c.</abbr> 1832)</figcaption></figure>
<p>Jackson saw his victory as a mandate to continue his war on the Bank's control over the national economy.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEllis197463_286-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEllis197463-286">&#91;285&#93;</a></sup> In 1833, Jackson signed an executive order ending the deposit of Treasury receipts in the bank.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKnodell2006542_287-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKnodell2006542-287">&#91;286&#93;</a></sup> When Secretary of the Treasury McLane refused to execute the order, Jackson replaced him with <a href="/wiki/William_J._Duane" title="William J. Duane">William J. Duane</a>, who also refused. Jackson then appointed <a href="/wiki/Roger_B._Taney" title="Roger B. Taney">Roger B. Taney</a> as acting secretary, who implemented Jackson's policy.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESchmidt1955328_288-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESchmidt1955328-288">&#91;287&#93;</a></sup> With the loss of federal deposits, the Bank had to contract its credit.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGatell196726_289-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGatell196726-289">&#91;288&#93;</a></sup> Biddle used this contraction to create an economic downturn in an attempt to get Jackson to compromise. Biddle wrote, "Nothing but the evidence of suffering abroad will produce any effect in Congress."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESchlesinger1945103_290-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESchlesinger1945103-290">&#91;289&#93;</a></sup> The attempt did not succeed, the economy recovered and Biddle was blamed for the recession.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHowe2007391–392_291-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHowe2007391–392-291">&#91;290&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Jackson's actions led those who disagreed with him to form the Whig Party. They claimed to oppose Jackson's expansion of executive power, calling him "<a href="/wiki/King_Andrew_the_First" title="King Andrew the First">King Andrew the First</a>", and naming their party after the <a href="/wiki/Whigs_(British_political_party)" title="Whigs (British political party)">English Whigs</a> who <a href="/wiki/Glorious_Revolution" title="Glorious Revolution">opposed</a> the British monarchy in the 17th century.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEllis197462_292-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEllis197462-292">&#91;291&#93;</a></sup> In March 1832, the Senate <a href="/wiki/Censure" title="Censure">censured</a> Jackson for inappropriately taking authority for the Treasury Department when it was the responsibility of Congress and refused to confirm Taney's appointment as secretary of the treasury.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEllis197454_293-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEllis197454-293">&#91;292&#93;</a></sup> In April, however, the House declared that the bank should not be rechartered. By July 1836, the Bank no longer held any federal deposits.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKnodell2006566_294-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKnodell2006566-294">&#91;293&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Jackson had Federal funds deposited into state banks friendly to the administration's policies, which critics called <a href="/wiki/Pet_banks" title="Pet banks">pet banks</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGatell196435–37_295-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGatell196435–37-295">&#91;294&#93;</a></sup> The number of these state banks more than doubled during Jackson's administration,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESchmidt1955328_288-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESchmidt1955328-288">&#91;287&#93;</a></sup> and investment patterns changed. The Bank, which had been the federal government's fiscal agent, invested heavily in <a href="/wiki/Trade_finance" title="Trade finance">trade</a> and financed interregional and international trade. State banks were more responsive to state governments, and invested heavily in <a href="/wiki/Equity_(finance)" title="Equity (finance)">land development</a>, land speculation, and state public works projects.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKnodell2006562–563_296-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKnodell2006562–563-296">&#91;295&#93;</a></sup> In spite of the efforts of Taney's successor, <a href="/wiki/Levi_Woodbury" title="Levi Woodbury">Levi Woodbury</a>, to control them, the pet banks expanded their loans, helping to create a speculative boom in the final years of Jackson's administration.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHowe2007393_297-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHowe2007393-297">&#91;296&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In January 1835, Jackson paid off the national debt, the only time in U.S. history that it had been accomplished.<sup id="cite_ref-npr_298-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-npr-298">&#91;297&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-299" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-299">&#91;298&#93;</a></sup> It was paid down through tariff revenues,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELatner2002113_282-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELatner2002113-282">&#91;281&#93;</a></sup> carefully managing federal funding of internal improvements like roads and canals,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHowe2007358–360_300-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHowe2007358–360-300">&#91;299&#93;</a></sup> and the sale of public lands.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHowe2007396_301-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHowe2007396-301">&#91;300&#93;</a></sup> Between 1834 and 1836, the government had unprecedented spike in land sales:<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERousseau2002460–461_302-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERousseau2002460–461-302">&#91;301&#93;</a></sup> At its peak in 1836, the profits from land sales were eight to twelve times higher than a typical year.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETimberlake1965412_303-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETimberlake1965412-303">&#91;302&#93;</a></sup> During Jackson's presidency, 63 million acres of public land—about the size of the state of Oklahoma—was sold.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESchmidt1955325_304-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESchmidt1955325-304">&#91;303&#93;</a></sup> After Jackson stepped down from the presidency in 1837, a Democrat-majority Senate <a href="/wiki/Expungement" title="Expungement">expunged</a> Jackson's censure.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERemini1984377_305-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERemini1984377-305">&#91;304&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEUS_Senate1837_306-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEUS_Senate1837-306">&#91;305&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Panic_of_1837">Panic of 1837</span></h4>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Panic_of_1837" title="Panic of 1837">Panic of 1837</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:The_times_panic_1837.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="alt=Political cartoon showing people suffering from economic trouble" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9c/The_times_panic_1837.jpg/220px-The_times_panic_1837.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="152" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9c/The_times_panic_1837.jpg/330px-The_times_panic_1837.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9c/The_times_panic_1837.jpg/440px-The_times_panic_1837.jpg 2x" data-file-width="5255" data-file-height="3639" /></a><figcaption>Lithograph cartoon of the Panic of 1837 published by <a href="/wiki/Henry_R._Robinson_(publisher)" title="Henry R. Robinson (publisher)">H. R. Robinson</a> (1837). Jackson is symbolized by "Glory" in the sky with top hat, spectacles, and pipe.</figcaption></figure>
<p>Despite the economic boom following Jackson's victory in the Bank War, land speculation in the west caused the <a href="/wiki/Panic_of_1837" title="Panic of 1837">Panic of 1837</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEOlson2002190_307-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEOlson2002190-307">&#91;306&#93;</a></sup>  Jackson's transfer of federal monies to state banks in 1833 caused western banks to relax their lending standards;<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERousseau2002459–460_308-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERousseau2002459–460-308">&#91;307&#93;</a></sup> the Indian Removal Act made large amounts of former Native American lands available for purchase and speculation.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEParinsLittlefield2011&#91;httpsbooksgooglecombooksidA7OlZPtIMy4CpgPR14_xiv&#93;_309-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEParinsLittlefield2011[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidA7OlZPtIMy4CpgPR14_xiv]-309">&#91;308&#93;</a></sup> Two of Jackson's acts in 1836 contributed to the Panic of 1837. One was the <a href="/wiki/Specie_Circular" title="Specie Circular">Specie Circular</a>, which mandated western lands only be purchased by money backed by <a href="/wiki/Bullion_coin" title="Bullion coin">specie</a>. The act was intended to stabilize the economy by reducing speculation on credit, but it caused a drain of gold and silver from the Eastern banks to the Western banks to address the needs of financing land transactions.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMcGrane196560–62_310-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMcGrane196560–62-310">&#91;309&#93;</a></sup> The other was the Deposit and Distribution Act, which transferred federal monies from eastern to western state banks.  Together, they left Eastern banks unable to pay specie to the British when they recalled their loans to address their economic problems in international trade.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERousseau200248_311-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERousseau200248-311">&#91;310&#93;</a></sup> The panic drove the U.S. economy into a depression that lasted until 1841.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEOlson2002190_307-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEOlson2002190-307">&#91;306&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Physical_assault_and_assassination_attempt">Physical assault and assassination attempt</span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Assassination_attempt_on_Jackson_(cropped)_(cropped).jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="Several people in a crowd, man aims a gun at Jackson" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/db/Assassination_attempt_on_Jackson_%28cropped%29_%28cropped%29.jpg/220px-Assassination_attempt_on_Jackson_%28cropped%29_%28cropped%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="137" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/db/Assassination_attempt_on_Jackson_%28cropped%29_%28cropped%29.jpg/330px-Assassination_attempt_on_Jackson_%28cropped%29_%28cropped%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/db/Assassination_attempt_on_Jackson_%28cropped%29_%28cropped%29.jpg/440px-Assassination_attempt_on_Jackson_%28cropped%29_%28cropped%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="739" data-file-height="460" /></a><figcaption>Lithograph of attempted assassination of Andrew Jackson published by Endicott &amp; Co. (1835)</figcaption></figure>
<p>Jackson was the first president to be subjected to physical assault as well an assassination attempt.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENester20132_312-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENester20132-312">&#91;311&#93;</a></sup> On May 6, 1833, Robert B. Randolph struck Jackson in the face with his hand because Jackson had ordered Randolph's dismissal from the navy for <a href="/wiki/Embezzlement" title="Embezzlement">embezzlement</a>. Jackson declined to press charges.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERemini198460–61_313-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERemini198460–61-313">&#91;312&#93;</a></sup> While leaving the <a href="/wiki/United_States_Capitol" title="United States Capitol">United States Capitol</a> on January 30, 1835, <a href="/wiki/Richard_Lawrence_(failed_assassin)" title="Richard Lawrence (failed assassin)">Richard Lawrence</a>, an unemployed house painter from England, aimed a pistol at Jackson, which misfired. Lawrence pulled out a second pistol, which also misfired. Jackson attacked Lawrence with his cane until others intervened to restrain Lawrence, who was later found not guilty by reason of insanity and institutionalized.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEJackson1967_314-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEJackson1967-314">&#91;313&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-AmericanHeritage.com_315-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-AmericanHeritage.com-315">&#91;314&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Slavery">Slavery</span></h3>
<p>During Jackson's presidency, slavery remained a political issue.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMcFaul197525_316-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMcFaul197525-316">&#91;315&#93;</a></sup> Though federal troops were used to crush <a href="/wiki/Nat_Turner%27s_slave_rebellion" title="Nat Turner&#39;s slave rebellion">Nat Turner's slave rebellion</a> in 1831,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAptheker1943300_317-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAptheker1943300-317">&#91;316&#93;</a></sup> Jackson ordered them withdrawn immediately afterwards despite the petition of local citizens for them to remain for protection.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBreen2015105–106_318-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBreen2015105–106-318">&#91;317&#93;</a></sup> Jackson considered the issue too divisive to the nation and to the delicate alliances of the Democratic Party,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELatner2002117_319-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELatner2002117-319">&#91;318&#93;</a></sup> while sympathetic newspapers argued for excluding slavery from federal politics and keeping it at the state level.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMcFaul197526–28_320-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMcFaul197526–28-320">&#91;319&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Jackson's view was challenged when the <a href="/wiki/American_Anti-Slavery_Society" title="American Anti-Slavery Society">American Anti-Slavery Society</a> formally agitated for <a href="/wiki/Abolitionism_in_the_United_States" title="Abolitionism in the United States">abolition</a><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHenig196943_321-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHenig196943-321">&#91;320&#93;</a></sup> by sending anti-slavery tracts through the postal system into the South in 1835.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELatner2002117_319-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELatner2002117-319">&#91;318&#93;</a></sup> Jackson condemned these agitators as "monsters"<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHenig196943–44_322-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHenig196943–44-322">&#91;321&#93;</a></sup> who should atone with their lives<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERemini1984260_323-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERemini1984260-323">&#91;322&#93;</a></sup> because they were attempting to destroy the Union by encouraging <a href="/wiki/Sectionalism" title="Sectionalism">sectionalism</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBrands2005554_324-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrands2005554-324">&#91;323&#93;</a></sup> The act provoked riots in Charleston, and pro-slavery Southerners demanded that the postal service ban distribution of the materials. To address the issue, Jackson authorized that the tracts could be sent only to subscribers, whose names could be made publicly accountable.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERemini1984258–260_325-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERemini1984258–260-325">&#91;324&#93;</a></sup> That December, Jackson called on Congress to prohibit the circulation through the South of "incendiary publications intended to instigate the slaves to insurrection".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERemini1984261_326-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERemini1984261-326">&#91;325&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Foreign_affairs">Foreign affairs</span></h3>
<div class="thumb tleft">
<div class="thumbinner" style="width: 232px;">
<div class="thumbimage" style="width: 230px; height: 250px; overflow: hidden;">
<div style="position: relative; top: -75px; left: -45px; width: 325px"><div class="noresize"><span typeof="mw:File"><a href="/wiki/File:JACKSON,_Andrew-President_(BEP_engraved_portrait).jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="Engraved portrait of Jackson as president by the Bureau of Engraving and Printing. This portrait has appeared on the $20 bill since 1929.[326]" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/24/JACKSON%2C_Andrew-President_%28BEP_engraved_portrait%29.jpg/325px-JACKSON%2C_Andrew-President_%28BEP_engraved_portrait%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="325" height="386" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/24/JACKSON%2C_Andrew-President_%28BEP_engraved_portrait%29.jpg/488px-JACKSON%2C_Andrew-President_%28BEP_engraved_portrait%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/24/JACKSON%2C_Andrew-President_%28BEP_engraved_portrait%29.jpg/650px-JACKSON%2C_Andrew-President_%28BEP_engraved_portrait%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="7636" data-file-height="9066" /></a></span></div></div>
</div>
<div class="thumbcaption">
<div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:JACKSON,_Andrew-President_(BEP_engraved_portrait).jpg" title="File:JACKSON, Andrew-President (BEP engraved portrait).jpg"> </a></div>Engraved portrait of Jackson as president by the <a href="/wiki/Bureau_of_Engraving_and_Printing" title="Bureau of Engraving and Printing">Bureau of Engraving and Printing</a>.
