This portrait has appeared on the <a href="/wiki/United_States_twenty-dollar_bill" title="United States twenty-dollar bill">$20 bill</a> since 1929.<sup id="cite_ref-1929$20Bill_327-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-1929$20Bill-327">&#91;326&#93;</a></sup>
</div>
</div>
</div>
<p>The Jackson administration successfully negotiated trade agreements with Great Britain, <a href="/wiki/History_of_Spain_(1808%E2%80%931874)" title="History of Spain (1808–1874)">Spain</a>, <a href="/wiki/Russian_Empire" title="Russian Empire">Russia</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Ottoman_Empire" title="Ottoman Empire">Ottoman Empire</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Rattanakosin_Kingdom_(1782%E2%80%931932)" title="Rattanakosin Kingdom (1782–1932)">Siam</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELatner2002120_328-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELatner2002120-328">&#91;327&#93;</a></sup> In his First Annual Message to Congress, Jackson addressed the issues of <a href="/wiki/Looting" title="Looting">spoliation</a> claims, demands of compensation for the capture of American ships and sailors by foreign nations during the <a href="/wiki/Napoleonic_Wars" title="Napoleonic Wars">Napoleonic Wars</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEThomas197651_329-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEThomas197651-329">&#91;328&#93;</a></sup> Using a combination of bluster and tact, he successfully settled these claims with Denmark, <a href="/wiki/Kingdom_of_Portugal" title="Kingdom of Portugal">Portugal</a>, and Spain,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELatner2002120_328-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELatner2002120-328">&#91;327&#93;</a></sup> but he had difficulty collecting spoliation claims from France, which was unwilling to pay an indemnity agreed to in an earlier treaty. Jackson asked Congress in 1834 to authorize reprisals against French property if the country failed to make payment, as well as to arm for defense.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEThomas197651_329-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEThomas197651-329">&#91;328&#93;</a></sup> In response, France put its Caribbean fleet on a wartime footing.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHowe2007263_330-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHowe2007263-330">&#91;329&#93;</a></sup> Both sides wanted to avoid a conflict, but the French wanted an apology for Jackson's belligerence. In his 1835 Annual Message to the Congress, Jackson asserted that he refused to apologize, but stated that he did not intend to "menace or insult the Government of France".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEThomas197663_331-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEThomas197663-331">&#91;330&#93;</a></sup> The French were assuaged and agreed to pay $5,000,000 (equivalent to $141,838,700&#32;in 2022) to settle the claims.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERemini1984288_332-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERemini1984288-332">&#91;331&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Since the early 1820s, large numbers of Americans had been immigrating into Texas, a territory of the <a href="/wiki/Mexican_War_of_Independence" title="Mexican War of Independence">newly independent</a> nation of <a href="/wiki/Mexico" title="Mexico">Mexico</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHowe2007658–659_333-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHowe2007658–659-333">&#91;332&#93;</a></sup> As early as 1824, Jackson had expressed a desire to acquire the region for the United States.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEStenberg1934229_334-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEStenberg1934229-334">&#91;333&#93;</a></sup> In 1829, he attempted to purchase it, but Mexico did not want to sell. By 1830, there were twice as many settlers from the United States as from Mexico, leading to tensions with the Mexican government that started the <a href="/wiki/Texas_Revolution" title="Texas Revolution">Texas Revolution</a>. During the conflict, Jackson covertly allowed the settlers to obtain weapons and money from the United States.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHowe2007659–669_335-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHowe2007659–669-335">&#91;334&#93;</a></sup> They defeated the Mexican military in April 1836 and soon afterward declared the region an independent country, the <a href="/wiki/Republic_of_Texas" title="Republic of Texas">Republic of Texas</a>. The new Republic asked Jackson to recognize and annex it. Although Jackson wanted to do so, he was hesitant because he was unsure it could maintain independence from Mexico.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELatner2002120_328-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELatner2002120-328">&#91;327&#93;</a></sup> He also was concerned because Texas had legalized slavery, which was an issue that could divide the Democrats during the 1836 election. Jackson recognized the Republic of Texas on the last full day of his presidency, March 3, 1837.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHowe2007670–671_336-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHowe2007670–671-336">&#91;335&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Judicial_appointments">Judicial appointments</span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/List_of_federal_judges_appointed_by_Andrew_Jackson" title="List of federal judges appointed by Andrew Jackson">List of federal judges appointed by Andrew Jackson</a></div>
<p>Jackson appointed six justices to the Supreme Court.<sup id="cite_ref-337" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-337">&#91;336&#93;</a></sup> Most were undistinguished. Jackson nominated Roger B. Taney in January 1835 to the Court in reward for his services, but the nomination failed to win Senate approval.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERemini1984266_338-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERemini1984266-338">&#91;337&#93;</a></sup> When Chief Justice Marshall died in 1835, Jackson nominated Taney for Chief Justice; he was confirmed by the new Senate,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERemini1984266–268_339-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERemini1984266–268-339">&#91;338&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Taney_Court" title="Taney Court">serving</a> as Chief Justice until 1864.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESchwartz199373–74_340-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESchwartz199373–74-340">&#91;339&#93;</a></sup> He was regarded with respect during his career on the bench, but he is most remembered for his decision in <i><a href="/wiki/Dred_Scott_v._Sandford" title="Dred Scott v. Sandford">Dred Scott v. Sandford</a></i>.<sup id="cite_ref-341" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-341">&#91;340&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="States_admitted_to_the_Union">States admitted to the Union</span></h3>
<p>Two new states were admitted into the Union during Jackson's presidency: Arkansas (June 15, 1836)<sup id="cite_ref-342" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-342">&#91;341&#93;</a></sup> and Michigan (January 26, 1837).<sup id="cite_ref-343" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-343">&#91;342&#93;</a></sup> Both states increased Democratic power in Congress and helped Van Buren win the presidency in 1836, as new states tended to support the party that had done the most to admit them.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERemini1984375–376_344-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERemini1984375–376-344">&#91;343&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span id="Later_life_and_death_.281837.E2.80.931845.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Later_life_and_death_(1837–1845)">Later life and death (1837–1845)</span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Andrew_Jackson_Daguerrotype_(flipped_image).jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a5/Andrew_Jackson_Daguerrotype_%28flipped_image%29.jpg/220px-Andrew_Jackson_Daguerrotype_%28flipped_image%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="250" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a5/Andrew_Jackson_Daguerrotype_%28flipped_image%29.jpg/330px-Andrew_Jackson_Daguerrotype_%28flipped_image%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a5/Andrew_Jackson_Daguerrotype_%28flipped_image%29.jpg/440px-Andrew_Jackson_Daguerrotype_%28flipped_image%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="4112" data-file-height="4674" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Mezzotint" title="Mezzotint">Mezzotint</a> of Jackson (1845)</figcaption></figure>
<p>In 1837, Jackson retired to the Hermitage and immediately began putting its affairs in order, as it had been poorly managed in his absence. Though Jackson was in ill health and had lost some of his popularity because he was blamed for the Panic of 1837, he remained influential in national and state politics.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELatner2002121_345-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELatner2002121-345">&#91;344&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Jackson supported an <a href="/wiki/Independent_Treasury" title="Independent Treasury">Independent Treasury</a> system as a solution to the panic, which would hold the money balances of the government in the form of gold or silver and would be restricted from printing <a href="/wiki/Banknote" title="Banknote">paper money</a> to prevent further <a href="/wiki/Inflation" title="Inflation">inflation</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELansfordWoods20081046_346-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELansfordWoods20081046-346">&#91;345&#93;</a></sup> This system was implemented in 1846.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETimberlake196093–98_347-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETimberlake196093–98-347">&#91;346&#93;</a></sup> The depression still continued, and Van Buren became unpopular. The Whig Party nominated war hero <a href="/wiki/William_Henry_Harrison" title="William Henry Harrison">William Henry Harrison</a> and former Democrat <a href="/wiki/John_Tyler" title="John Tyler">John Tyler</a> for the <a href="/wiki/1840_United_States_presidential_election" title="1840 United States presidential election">1840 presidential election</a>. They used a campaign style similar to that of the Democrats: Van Buren was depicted as an uncaring aristocrat, while Harrison's war record was glorified, and he was portrayed as a man of the people.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBrands2005475_348-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrands2005475-348">&#91;347&#93;</a></sup> Jackson campaigned loyally for Van Buren in Tennessee.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERemini1984462–470_349-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERemini1984462–470-349">&#91;348&#93;</a></sup> He favored <a href="/wiki/James_K._Polk" title="James K. Polk">James K. Polk</a> as vice presidential candidate, but no candidate for that office was chosen.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERemini1984463–464_350-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERemini1984463–464-350">&#91;349&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>To Jackson's dismay, Harrison won the 1840 election with the Whigs capturing majorities in both houses of Congress.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERemini1984470_351-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERemini1984470-351">&#91;350&#93;</a></sup> Harrison died only a month into his term, and was replaced by Tyler. Jackson was encouraged because Tyler was not bound to party loyalties. Tyler angered the Whigs in 1841 when he vetoed two Whig-sponsored bills to establish a new national bank. Jackson and other Democrats praised Tyler,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERemini1984475–476_352-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERemini1984475–476-352">&#91;351&#93;</a></sup> but Tyler's entire cabinet, except Daniel Webster, resigned.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMcCormick2002143_353-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMcCormick2002143-353">&#91;352&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Andrew_Jackson_Tomb.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="A tomb in a garden covered by a circular roof" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/58/Andrew_Jackson_Tomb.jpg/170px-Andrew_Jackson_Tomb.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="230" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/58/Andrew_Jackson_Tomb.jpg/255px-Andrew_Jackson_Tomb.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/58/Andrew_Jackson_Tomb.jpg/340px-Andrew_Jackson_Tomb.jpg 2x" data-file-width="406" data-file-height="550" /></a><figcaption>The tomb of Andrew and <a href="/wiki/Rachel_Jackson" title="Rachel Jackson">Rachel Jackson</a> at <a href="/wiki/The_Hermitage_(Nashville,_Tennessee)" title="The Hermitage (Nashville, Tennessee)">The Hermitage</a>, designed by David Morrison (1832)</figcaption></figure>
<p>Jackson lobbied for the <a href="/wiki/Texas_annexation" title="Texas annexation">annexation of Texas</a>, insisting that it belonged to the United States as part of the <a href="/wiki/Louisiana_Purchase" title="Louisiana Purchase">Louisiana Purchase</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERemini1984492_354-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERemini1984492-354">&#91;353&#93;</a></sup> He thought that annexation would cause national division over slavery, but feared the British could use Texas as a base to threaten the United States.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWilentz2005161–163_355-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilentz2005161–163-355">&#91;354&#93;</a></sup> Jackson wrote several letters to Texas president <a href="/wiki/Sam_Houston" title="Sam Houston">Sam Houston</a>, urging him to wait for the Senate to approve annexation and explaining how much Texas would benefit as a part of the United States.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERemini1984493_356-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERemini1984493-356">&#91;355&#93;</a></sup> Tyler signed a treaty of annexation in April 1844, but it became associated with the expansion of slavery and was not ratified. Henry Clay, the Whig nominee for the 1844 presidential election, and Van Buren, Jackson's preferred candidate for the Democratic Party, both opposed annexation. Disappointed by Van Buren, Jackson convinced Polk, who was to be Van Buren's running mate, to run as the Democratic Party's presidential nominee instead. Polk defeated Van Buren for the nomination, and Jackson convinced Tyler not to run as an independent by bringing him back into the Democratic Party. Polk won the election, the Senate passed a bill to annex Texas, and it was signed on March 1, 1845.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWilentz2005163_357-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilentz2005163-357">&#91;356&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Jackson died of <a href="/wiki/Edema" title="Edema">dropsy</a>, <a href="/wiki/Tuberculosis" title="Tuberculosis">tuberculosis</a> and <a href="/wiki/Heart_failure" title="Heart failure">heart failure</a><sup id="cite_ref-healthguidance_358-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-healthguidance-358">&#91;357&#93;</a></sup> at 78 years of age on June 8, 1845. He was surrounded by family and friends at his deathbed, and his last words were, "Oh, do not cry. Be good children and we will all meet in Heaven."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMeacham2008345_359-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMeacham2008345-359">&#91;358&#93;</a></sup> He was buried in the same tomb as his wife Rachel.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERemini1984526_360-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERemini1984526-360">&#91;359&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Personal_life">Personal life</span></h2>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Family">Family</span></h3>
<p>Jackson and Rachel had no children together but adopted Andrew Jackson Jr., the son of Rachel's deceased brother Severn Donelson. The Jacksons acted as guardians for Donelson's other children: John Samuel, <a href="/wiki/Daniel_Smith_Donelson" title="Daniel Smith Donelson">Daniel Smith</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Andrew_Jackson_Donelson" title="Andrew Jackson Donelson">Andrew Jackson</a>. They were also guardians for Andrew Jackson Hutchings, Rachel's orphaned grand nephew, and the orphaned children of a friend, Edward Butler – Caroline, Eliza, Edward, and Anthony – who lived with the Jacksons after their father died.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERemini1977180–161_361-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERemini1977180–161-361">&#91;360&#93;</a></sup> Jackson also had three Creek children living with them: <a href="/wiki/Lyncoya_Jackson" title="Lyncoya Jackson">Lyncoya</a>, a Creek orphan Jackson had adopted after the Battle of Tallushatchee,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERemini1977194_362-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERemini1977194-362">&#91;361&#93;</a></sup> and two boys they called Theodore<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMoserMacpherson1984444,_fn_5_363-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMoserMacpherson1984444,_fn_5-363">&#91;362&#93;</a></sup> and Charley.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMoserHothMacphersonReinbold199160,_fn_3_364-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMoserHothMacphersonReinbold199160,_fn_3-364">&#91;363&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Tennessee_Gentleman_portrait_of_Andrew_Jackson_by_Ralph_E._W._Earl.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="Painting of a man with a tall white hat, cane, black pants and coat, and a white shirt. He is standing on grass beside a tree." src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/37/Tennessee_Gentleman_portrait_of_Andrew_Jackson_by_Ralph_E._W._Earl.jpg/220px-Tennessee_Gentleman_portrait_of_Andrew_Jackson_by_Ralph_E._W._Earl.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="293" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/37/Tennessee_Gentleman_portrait_of_Andrew_Jackson_by_Ralph_E._W._Earl.jpg/330px-Tennessee_Gentleman_portrait_of_Andrew_Jackson_by_Ralph_E._W._Earl.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/37/Tennessee_Gentleman_portrait_of_Andrew_Jackson_by_Ralph_E._W._Earl.jpg/440px-Tennessee_Gentleman_portrait_of_Andrew_Jackson_by_Ralph_E._W._Earl.jpg 2x" data-file-width="700" data-file-height="931" /></a><figcaption>Jackson as a <i>Tennessee Gentleman</i> by <a href="/wiki/Ralph_Eleaser_Whiteside_Earl" title="Ralph Eleaser Whiteside Earl">Ralph E. W. Earl</a> ( c. 1831, <a href="/wiki/The_Hermitage_(Nashville,_Tennessee)" title="The Hermitage (Nashville, Tennessee)">the Hermitage, Tennessee</a>)</figcaption></figure>
<p>For the only time in U.S. history, two women acted simultaneously as unofficial first lady for the widower Jackson. Rachel's niece <a href="/wiki/Emily_Donelson" title="Emily Donelson">Emily Donelson</a> was married to Andrew Jackson Donelson (who acted as Jackson's private secretary) and served as hostess at the White House. The president and Emily became estranged for over a year during the Petticoat affair, but they eventually reconciled and she resumed her duties as White House hostess. <a href="/wiki/Sarah_Yorke_Jackson" title="Sarah Yorke Jackson">Sarah Yorke Jackson</a>, the wife of Andrew Jackson Jr., became co-hostess of the White House in 1834, and took over all hostess duties after Emily died from tuberculosis in 1836.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMeacham2008109,_315_365-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMeacham2008109,_315-365">&#91;364&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Temperament">Temperament</span></h3>
<p>Jackson had a reputation for being short-tempered and violent,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESomit1948295_366-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESomit1948295-366">&#91;365&#93;</a></sup> which terrified his opponents.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBrands2005297_367-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrands2005297-367">&#91;366&#93;</a></sup> He was able to use his temper strategically to accomplish what he wanted.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMeacham200837Remini19777Wilentz20053_368-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMeacham200837Remini19777Wilentz20053-368">&#91;367&#93;</a></sup> He could keep it in check when necessary: his behavior was friendly and urbane when he went to Washington as senator during the campaign leading up to the 1824 election. According to Van Buren, he remained calm in times of difficulty and made his decisions deliberatively.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESomit1948302_369-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESomit1948302-369">&#91;368&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>He had the tendency to take things personally. If someone crossed him, he would often become obsessed with crushing them.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESomit1948297–300_370-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESomit1948297–300-370">&#91;369&#93;</a></sup> For example, on the last day of his presidency, Jackson declared he had only two regrets: that he had not hanged Henry Clay or shot John C. Calhoun.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBorneman200836_371-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBorneman200836-371">&#91;370&#93;</a></sup> He also had a strong sense of loyalty. He considered threats to his friends as threats to himself, but he demanded unquestioning loyalty in return.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESomit1948306_372-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESomit1948306-372">&#91;371&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Jackson was self-confident,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMeacham200819_373-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMeacham200819-373">&#91;372&#93;</a></sup> without projecting a sense of self-importance.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESomit1948299–300_374-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESomit1948299–300-374">&#91;373&#93;</a></sup> This self-confidence gave him the ability to persevere in the face of adversity.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERemini1977178–_179_375-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERemini1977178–_179-375">&#91;374&#93;</a></sup> Once he decided on a plan of action, he would adhere to it.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESomit1948312_376-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESomit1948312-376">&#91;375&#93;</a></sup> His reputation for being both quick-tempered and confident worked to his advantage;<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBrown2022&#91;httpsbooksgooglecombooksid3t9IEAAAQBAJpgPA191_191&#93;_377-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrown2022[httpsbooksgooglecombooksid3t9IEAAAQBAJpgPA191_191]-377">&#91;376&#93;</a></sup> it misled opponents to see him as simple and direct, leading them to often understimate his political shrewdness.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESomit1948304_378-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESomit1948304-378">&#91;377&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Religious_faith">Religious faith</span></h3>
<p>In 1838, Jackson became an official member of the <a href="/wiki/Downtown_Presbyterian_Church_(Nashville)" title="Downtown Presbyterian Church (Nashville)">First Presbyterian Church</a> in Nashville.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWilentz2005160_379-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilentz2005160-379">&#91;378&#93;</a></sup> Both his mother and his wife had been devout Presbyterians all their lives, but Jackson stated that he had postponed officially entering the church until after his retirement to avoid accusations that he had done so for political reasons.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERemini1984444_380-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERemini1984444-380">&#91;379&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Legacy">Legacy</span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Andrew_Jackson_statue_County_Courthouse_KC_Missouri.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="Statue of a man on a horse in front of a large stone building" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/7c/Andrew_Jackson_statue_County_Courthouse_KC_Missouri.jpg/220px-Andrew_Jackson_statue_County_Courthouse_KC_Missouri.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="289" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/7c/Andrew_Jackson_statue_County_Courthouse_KC_Missouri.jpg/330px-Andrew_Jackson_statue_County_Courthouse_KC_Missouri.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/7c/Andrew_Jackson_statue_County_Courthouse_KC_Missouri.jpg/440px-Andrew_Jackson_statue_County_Courthouse_KC_Missouri.jpg 2x" data-file-width="653" data-file-height="857" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Equestrian_statue" title="Equestrian statue">Equestrian statue</a> of Jackson by <a href="/wiki/Charles_Keck" title="Charles Keck">Charles Keck</a> in front of the Jackson County Courthouse, <a href="/wiki/Kansas_City,_Missouri" title="Kansas City, Missouri">Kansas City, Missouri</a> (1934). Commissioned by Judge <a href="/wiki/Harry_S._Truman" title="Harry S. Truman">Harry S. Truman</a></figcaption></figure>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">See also: <a href="/wiki/List_of_memorials_to_Andrew_Jackson" title="List of memorials to Andrew Jackson">List of memorials to Andrew Jackson</a></div>
<p>Jackson's legacy is controversial<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAdams2013&#91;httpsbooksgooglecombooksid_z2JMnBpTqgCpgPA1_1–2&#93;_381-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAdams2013[httpsbooksgooglecombooksid_z2JMnBpTqgCpgPA1_1–2]-381">&#91;380&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-JacksonsShiftingLegacy_382-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-JacksonsShiftingLegacy-382">&#91;381&#93;</a></sup> and polarized.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESellers1958615_383-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESellers1958615-383">&#91;382&#93;</a></sup> Jackson's contemporary, <a href="/wiki/Alexis_de_Tocqueville" title="Alexis de Tocqueville">Alexis de Tocqueville</a>, depicted Jackson as the spokesperson of the majority and their passions.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETocqueville1840392–394_384-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETocqueville1840392–394-384">&#91;383&#93;</a></sup> He has been variously described as a frontiersman personifying the independence of the American West,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETurner1920252–254_385-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETurner1920252–254-385">&#91;384&#93;</a></sup> a slave-owning member of the Southern gentry,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECheathem2014a&#91;httpsbooksgooglecombooksidZdStAAAAQBAJpgPP19_Introduction,_§9&#93;_386-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECheathem2014a[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidZdStAAAAQBAJpgPP19_Introduction,_§9]-386">&#91;385&#93;</a></sup> and a <a href="/wiki/Populism" title="Populism">populist</a> who promoted faith in the wisdom of the ordinary citizen.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWatson2017218_387-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWatson2017218-387">&#91;386&#93;</a></sup> He has been represented as a statesman who substantially advanced the spirit of democracy<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERemini19906_388-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERemini19906-388">&#91;387&#93;</a></sup> and upheld the foundations of American <a href="/wiki/Constitutionalism" title="Constitutionalism">constitutionalism</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBrogdon2011273_389-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrogdon2011273-389">&#91;388&#93;</a></sup> as well as an autocratic <a href="/wiki/Demagogue" title="Demagogue">demagogue</a> who  crushed political opposition and trampled the law.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENester20132–3_390-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENester20132–3-390">&#91;389&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-391" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-391">&#91;390&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In the 1920s, Jackson's rise to power became associated with the idea of the "common man".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAdams2013&#91;httpsbooksgooglecombooksid_z2JMnBpTqgCpgPA8_8&#93;Ward196282_392-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAdams2013[httpsbooksgooglecombooksid_z2JMnBpTqgCpgPA8_8]Ward196282-392">&#91;391&#93;</a></sup> This idea defined the age as a populist rejection of social elites and a vindication of every person's value independent of class and status.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWard196282–83_393-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWard196282–83-393">&#91;392&#93;</a></sup> Jackson was seen as its personification,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMurphy2013&#91;httpsbooksgooglecombooksidnyIQMv2ODoMCpgPA261_261&#93;_394-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMurphy2013[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidnyIQMv2ODoMCpgPA261_261]-394">&#91;393&#93;</a></sup> an individual free of societal constraints who can achieve great things.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFish1927337-338_395-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFish1927337-338-395">&#91;394&#93;</a></sup> In 1945, <a href="/wiki/Arthur_M._Schlesinger_Jr." title="Arthur M. Schlesinger Jr.">Arthur M. Schlesinger Jr.</a>'s influential <i>Age of Jackson</i> redefined Jackson's legacy through the lens of <a href="/wiki/Franklin_D._Roosevelt" title="Franklin D. Roosevelt">Franklin D. Roosevelt</a>'s <a href="/wiki/New_Deal" title="New Deal">New Deal</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAdams2013&#91;httpsbooksgooglecombooksid_z2JMnBpTqgCpgPA3_3–4&#93;_396-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAdams2013[httpsbooksgooglecombooksid_z2JMnBpTqgCpgPA3_3–4]-396">&#91;395&#93;</a></sup> describing the common man as a member of the working class struggling against exploitation by business concerns.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECheathem20135Cole1986151_397-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECheathem20135Cole1986151-397">&#91;396&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In the twenty-first century, Jackson's Indian Removal Act has been described as <a href="/wiki/Ethnic_cleansing" title="Ethnic cleansing">ethnic cleansing</a>:<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAnderson2016416Carson20089–10Garrison20022–3Howe2007423Kakel2011&#91;httpsbooksgooglecombooksid7Nt8DAAAQBAJpgPA158_158&#93;Lynn2019&#91;httpsbooksgooglecombooksidbqOfDwAAQBAJpgPA78_78&#93;_398-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAnderson2016416Carson20089–10Garrison20022–3Howe2007423Kakel2011[httpsbooksgooglecombooksid7Nt8DAAAQBAJpgPA158_158]Lynn2019[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidbqOfDwAAQBAJpgPA78_78]-398">&#91;397&#93;</a></sup> the use of force, terror and violence to make an area ethnically homogeneous.<sup id="cite_ref-UNEthnicCleansing_399-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-UNEthnicCleansing-399">&#91;398&#93;</a></sup> To achieve the goal of separating Native Americans from the whites,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPerdue20126Remini199056–59_400-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPerdue20126Remini199056–59-400">&#91;399&#93;</a></sup> coercive force such as threats and bribes were used to effect removal <sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECave20031337Howe2007348_401-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECave20031337Howe2007348-401">&#91;400&#93;</a></sup> and unauthorized military force was used when there was resistance,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECave20031337_230-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECave20031337-230">&#91;229&#93;</a></sup> as in the case of the Second Seminole War.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMissallMissall2004xv–xvii_402-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMissallMissall2004xv–xvii-402">&#91;401&#93;</a></sup> The act has been discussed in the context of genocide,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGilo-Whitaker2019&#91;httpsbooksgooglecombooksidV9GLDwAAQBAJpgPA35_35–36&#93;_403-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGilo-Whitaker2019[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidV9GLDwAAQBAJpgPA35_35–36]-403">&#91;402&#93;</a></sup> and its role in the long-term <a href="/wiki/Genocide_of_indigenous_peoples#United_States_colonization_of_indigenous_territories" class="mw-redirect" title="Genocide of indigenous peoples">destruction of Native American societies</a> and their cultures continues to be debated.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEOstler2019&#91;httpsbooksgooglecombooksid6zeWDwAAQBAJpgPA365_365&#93;-&#91;httpsbooksgooglecombooksid6zeWDwAAQBAJpgPA366_366&#93;Perdue20123_404-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEOstler2019[httpsbooksgooglecombooksid6zeWDwAAQBAJpgPA365_365]-[httpsbooksgooglecombooksid6zeWDwAAQBAJpgPA366_366]Perdue20123-404">&#91;403&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>His legacy has been variously used by later presidents. <a href="/wiki/Abraham_Lincoln" title="Abraham Lincoln">Abraham Lincoln</a> referenced Jackson's ideas when negotiating the challenges to the Union that he faced during 1861, including Jackson's understanding of the constitution during the nullification crisis and the president's right to interpret the constitution.<sup id="cite_ref-WilentzLincoln_405-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-WilentzLincoln-405">&#91;404&#93;</a></sup> Franklin D. Roosevelt used Jackson to redefine the Democratic Party, describing him as a defender of the exploited and downtrodden and as a fighter for social justice and human rights.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBrands2008449–450_406-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrands2008449–450-406">&#91;405&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-RooseveltJackson_407-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-RooseveltJackson-407">&#91;406&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Donald_Trump" title="Donald Trump">Donald Trump</a> used Jackson's legacy to present himself as the president of the common man,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBrown2022&#91;httpsbooksgooglecombooksid3t9IEAAAQBAJpgPA367_367&#93;_408-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrown2022[httpsbooksgooglecombooksid3t9IEAAAQBAJpgPA367_367]-408">&#91;407&#93;</a></sup> praising Jackson for saving the country from a rising aristocracy and protecting American workers with a tariff.<sup id="cite_ref-409" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-409">&#91;408&#93;</a></sup> In 2016, President <a href="/wiki/Barack_Obama" title="Barack Obama">Barack Obama</a>'s administration announced it was removing Jackson's portrait from the $20 bill and replacing it with one of <a href="/wiki/Harriet_Tubman" title="Harriet Tubman">Harriet Tubman</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEThompsonBarchiesi20181_410-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEThompsonBarchiesi20181-410">&#91;409&#93;</a></sup> Though the plan was put on hold during Trump's presidency, President <a href="/wiki/Joe_Biden" title="Joe Biden">Joe Biden</a>'s administration resumed it in 2021.<sup id="cite_ref-411" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-411">&#91;410&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Jackson is usually rated highly as a president, but his reputation is dropping.<sup id="cite_ref-FellerLegacy_412-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FellerLegacy-412">&#91;411&#93;</a></sup> His contradictory legacy is shown in <a href="/wiki/Historical_rankings_of_presidents_of_the_United_States" title="Historical rankings of presidents of the United States">opinion polls</a>. A 2014 survey of political scientists rated Jackson as the ninth-highest rated president but the third-most polarizing. He was also rated the third-most overrated president.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERottinghausVaughn2017_413-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERottinghausVaughn2017-413">&#91;412&#93;</a></sup>  In a <a href="/wiki/C-SPAN" title="C-SPAN">C-SPAN</a> poll, Jackson was ranked the 13th in 2009, 18th in 2017, and 22nd in 2021.<sup id="cite_ref-CSPAN2021_414-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-CSPAN2021-414">&#91;413&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Writings">Writings</span></h2>

<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Notes">Notes</span></h2>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1011085734">.mw-parser-output .reflist{font-size:90%;margin-bottom:0.5em;list-style-type:decimal}.mw-parser-output .reflist .references{font-size:100%;margin-bottom:0;list-style-type:inherit}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns-2{column-width:30em}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns-3{column-width:25em}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns{margin-top:0.3em}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns ol{margin-top:0}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns li{page-break-inside:avoid;break-inside:avoid-column}.mw-parser-output .reflist-upper-alpha{list-style-type:upper-alpha}.mw-parser-output .reflist-upper-roman{list-style-type:upper-roman}.mw-parser-output .reflist-lower-alpha{list-style-type:lower-alpha}.mw-parser-output .reflist-lower-greek{list-style-type:lower-greek}.mw-parser-output .reflist-lower-roman{list-style-type:lower-roman}</style><div class="reflist reflist-lower-alpha">
<div class="mw-references-wrap"><ol class="references">
<li id="cite_note-1"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-1">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Vice President Calhoun resigned from office. As this was prior to the adoption of the <a href="/wiki/Twenty-fifth_Amendment_to_the_United_States_Constitution" title="Twenty-fifth Amendment to the United States Constitution">Twenty-fifth Amendment</a> in 1967, a vacancy in the office of vice president was not filled until the next ensuing election and inauguration.</span>
</li>
</ol></div></div>
